[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Kashmir_region._LOC_2003626427_-_showing_sub-regions_administered_by_different_countries.jpg/330px-Kashmir_region._LOC_2003626427_-_showing_sub-regions_administered_by_different_countries.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kashmir_region._LOC_2003626427_-_showing_sub-regions_administered_by_different_countries.jpg) India claims the entire erstwhile [British Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj "British Raj") [princely state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_state "Princely state") of [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_(princely_state) "Jammu and Kashmir (princely state)") based on an [instrument of accession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Accession_(Jammu_and_Kashmir) "Instrument of Accession (Jammu and Kashmir)") signed in 1947. Pakistan claims most of the region based on its [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslims "Muslims")-majority population, whereas China claims the largely uninhabited regions of [Aksai Chin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aksai_Chin "Aksai Chin") and the [Shaksgam Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Karakoram_Tract "Trans-Karakoram Tract").

"2025 India–Pakistan crisis") |

The **Kashmir conflict** is a territorial conflict over the [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir "Kashmir") region, primarily between [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India "India") and [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan "Pakistan"), and also between [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China") and India in the northeastern portion of the region.[&#8202;](#ref-back-1-0)[\[1\]](#refnote-1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-2-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-2) The conflict started after the [partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India "Partition of India") in 1947 as both India and Pakistan claimed the entirety of the former princely state of [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_(princely_state) "Jammu and Kashmir (princely state)"). It is a dispute over the region that escalated into three wars between India and Pakistan and several other armed skirmishes. India controls approximately 55% of the land area of the region that includes [Jammu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Division "Jammu Division"), the [Kashmir Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir_Valley "Kashmir Valley"), most of [Ladakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladakh "Ladakh"), the [Siachen Glacier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siachen_Glacier "Siachen Glacier"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-leess-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-leess)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kargil_from_Surprise_to_Victory-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-Kargil_from_Surprise_to_Victory) and 70% of its population; Pakistan controls approximately 30% of the land area that includes [Azad Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Kashmir "Azad Kashmir") and [Gilgit-Baltistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilgit-Baltistan "Gilgit-Baltistan"); and China controls the remaining 15% of the land area that includes the [Aksai Chin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aksai_Chin "Aksai Chin") region, the mostly uninhabited [Trans-Karakoram Tract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Karakoram_Tract "Trans-Karakoram Tract"), and part of the [Demchok sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demchok_sector "Demchok sector").[&#8202;](#ref-back-leess-1)[\[3\]](#refnote-leess)[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[note 1\]](#refnote-11)

After the partition of India and [a rebellion in the western districts of the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947_Poonch_rebellion "1947 Poonch rebellion"), Pakistani tribal militias invaded Kashmir, leading the Hindu ruler of Jammu and Kashmir to join India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-12) The resulting [Indo-Pakistani War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1947%E2%80%931948 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1947–1948") ended with a [UN-mediated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_mediation_of_the_Kashmir_dispute "UN mediation of the Kashmir dispute") ceasefire along a line that was eventually named the [Line of Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Control "Line of Control").[&#8202;](#ref-back-13-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-13)[&#8202;](#ref-back-britannica-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-britannica) In 1962, China invaded and fought a [war with India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_war "Sino-Indian war") along the [disputed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_border_dispute "Sino-Indian border dispute") Indo-Chinese border, including in Indian administered- [Ladakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladakh "Ladakh"), marking their entry to the Kashmir conflict.[&#8202;](#ref-back-15-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-15) In 1965, Pakistan attempted to infiltrate Indian-administered Kashmir to precipitate an insurgency there, resulting in [another war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Kashmir_War "Second Kashmir War") fought by the two countries over the region. After further fighting during the war of [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1971"), the [Simla Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_Agreement "Simla Agreement") formally established the Line of Control between the territories under Indian and Pakistani control.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MEA_site-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-MEA_site)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Fortna-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-Fortna) In 1999, an armed conflict between the two countries broke out again in [Kargil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_War "Kargil War") with no effect on the _status quo_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-18)

In 1989, an [armed insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurgency_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir "Insurgency in Jammu and Kashmir") erupted against Indian rule in Indian-administered Kashmir Valley, after years of political disenfranchisement and alienation, with logistical support from Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGanguly2016p._10:_"In_December_I989,_an_indigenous,_ethno-religious_insurgency_erupted_in_the_Indian-controlled_portion_of_the_disputed_state_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir._The_internal_dimensions_of_this_crisis,_like_that_in_the_Punjab,_also_stemmed_primarily_from_various_shortcomings_in_India's_federal_order."-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGanguly2016p._10:_"In_December_I989,_an_indigenous,_ethno-religious_insurgency_erupted_in_the_Indian-controlled_portion_of_the_disputed_state_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir._The_internal_dimensions_of_this_crisis,_like_that_in_the_Punjab,_also_stemmed_primarily_from_various_shortcomings_in_India's_federal_order.")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2006p._273:_"The_year_1989_marked_the_beginning_of_a_continuing_insurgency,_fuelled_by_covert_support_from_Pakistan._The_uprising_had_its_origins_in_Kashmiri_frustration_at_the_state’s_treatment_by_Delhi._The_imposition_of_leaders_chosen_by_the_centre,_with_the_manipulation_of_local_elections,_and_the_denial_of_what_Kashmiris_felt_was_a_promised_autonomy_boiled_over_at_last_in_the_militancy_of_the_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front,_a_movement_devoted_to_political,_not_religious,_objectives."-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2006p._273:_"The_year_1989_marked_the_beginning_of_a_continuing_insurgency,_fuelled_by_covert_support_from_Pakistan._The_uprising_had_its_origins_in_Kashmiri_frustration_at_the_state’s_treatment_by_Delhi._The_imposition_of_leaders_chosen_by_the_centre,_with_the_manipulation_of_local_elections,_and_the_denial_of_what_Kashmiris_felt_was_a_promised_autonomy_boiled_over_at_last_in_the_militancy_of_the_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front,_a_movement_devoted_to_political,_not_religious,_objectives.")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEHussain2018p._104:_"In_the_late_1980s,_a_small_group_of_Kashmiris_who_had_lost_faith_in_Indian_democracy_decided_to_take_the_long-standing_conflict_between_India_and_Pakistan_to_a_new_level._These_individuals,_mostly_jailed_MUF_political_activists,_collectively_decided_to_go_to_Pakistani-administered_Kashmir_in_search_of_training_and_weapons._Inspired_by_the_ideology_of_the_Jammu_Kashmir_Liberation_Front_(JKLF-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEHussain2018p._104:_"In_the_late_1980s,_a_small_group_of_Kashmiris_who_had_lost_faith_in_Indian_democracy_decided_to_take_the_long-standing_conflict_between_India_and_Pakistan_to_a_new_level._These_individuals,_mostly_jailed_MUF_political_activists,_collectively_decided_to_go_to_Pakistani-administered_Kashmir_in_search_of_training_and_weapons._Inspired_by_the_ideology_of_the_Jammu_Kashmir_Liberation_Front_(JKLF),_a_party_that_advocated_for_an_independent_Kashmir,_these_individuals,_with_the_support_of_Pakistan_intelligence_agencies,_initiated_an_armed_rebellion_in_the_Valley_and_popularized_the_slogan_of_aazadi_(Khan,_1992,_131%E2%80%9341).%22-21) The insurgency was actively opposed in Jammu and Ladakh, where it revived long-held demands for autonomy from Kashmiri dominance and greater integration with India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEZutshi2019p._133:_"Far_from_desiring_autonomy_from_India,_Jammu_and_Ladakhi_politics_was_based_on_demands_for_autonomy_from_Kashmir_and_its_repressive_governments_instead,_and_greater_integration_with_India._…_The_insurgency,_thus,_widened_the_divides_among_the_sub-regions_of_Kashmir,_the_long-term_repercussions_of_which_on_state_politics_are_only_recently_becoming_clearer."-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEZutshi2019p._133:_"Far_from_desiring_autonomy_from_India,_Jammu_and_Ladakhi_politics_was_based_on_demands_for_autonomy_from_Kashmir_and_its_repressive_governments_instead,_and_greater_integration_with_India._…_The_insurgency,_thus,_widened_the_divides_among_the_sub-regions_of_Kashmir,_the_long-term_repercussions_of_which_on_state_politics_are_only_recently_becoming_clearer.")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESnedden2021pp._5–6:_"The_JKLF’s_stance_was_popular_with_Muslim_Kashmiris,_many_of_whom_thought_that_independence_was_‘just_around_the_corner’._However,_this_possibility_was_not_popular_with_all_J&K-ites:_Hindu_Kashmiris_(called_Pandits-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESnedden2021pp._5–6:_"The_JKLF’s_stance_was_popular_with_Muslim_Kashmiris,_many_of_whom_thought_that_independence_was_‘just_around_the_corner’._However,_this_possibility_was_not_popular_with_all_J&K-ites:_Hindu_Kashmiris_(called_Pandits),_some_Muslim_Kashmiris,_and_many_people_located_in_Jammu_or_Ladakh_strongly_wanted_J&K_to_be_with_India._Some_Muslims_in_the_Kashmir_Valley_and_in_Jammu_wanted_J&K_to_join_Pakistan.%22-23)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBehera2006(p._115-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBehera2006(p._115)_%22The_winter_of_1989%E2%80%9390_marked_the_onset_of_the_Kashmiri_insurgency_%E2%80%A6_while_the_Ladakhi_Buddhists_began_their_violent_agitation_for_status_as_a_union_territory_in_August_1989._The_next_few_years_witnessed_a_growing_communalization_of_the_political_idiom,_strategies,_and_goals_of_various_political_movements_in_the_state._Where_the_Kashmiris_cast_their_demand_for_secession_in_terms_of_a_Hindu-Muslim_divide,_especially_after_the_Pandit_exodus_in_1990,_the_Buddhists_mobilized_against_the_Kashmiris_on_the_basis_of_a_Buddhist-Muslim_divide,_which_they_also_extended_to_the_Shias_of_Leh,_who_are_almost_all_of_Balti_stock_and_ethnically_similar_to_Ladakhi_Buddhists.%22_(p._122)_%22After_suffering_political_and_economic_neglect_at_the_hands_of_successive_state_governments,_Jammu_began_making_demands_again_as_well._These_ranged_from_a_separate_state_of_Jammu_to_regional_autonomy_and_a_regional_council._Significantly,_the_proposals_were_all_rooted_in_Jammu%E2%80%99s_regional_aspirations,_while_the)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield2003pp._184–185:"Neither_the_Buddhists_of_Ladakh_nor_the_Hindus_of_Jammu_share_the_objectives_of_the_Muslim_Kashmiris_of_the_valley._Their_main_concern_has_been_to_press_for_autonomy_against_dominance_from_the_more_populous_valley._…_In_Ladakh,_the_troubles_between_Muslims_of_the_Kargil_district_and_Buddhists_which_erupted_in_1989_have_now_subsided._…_However,_even_the_Muslims_of_Jammu,_who_are_not_Kashmiri_speaking,_do_not_necessarily_support_the_demands_of_the_valley_Kashmiri_Muslims._…_Mistrust,_however,_remains_between_Muslims_and_the_displaced_Kashmiri_Pandits,_some_of_whom_are_now_demanding_a_separate_homeland_in_the_valley_for_the_700,000_Pandits_living_in_different_parts_of_India."-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield2003pp._184–185:"Neither_the_Buddhists_of_Ladakh_nor_the_Hindus_of_Jammu_share_the_objectives_of_the_Muslim_Kashmiris_of_the_valley._Their_main_concern_has_been_to_press_for_autonomy_against_dominance_from_the_more_populous_valley._…_In_Ladakh,_the_troubles_between_Muslims_of_the_Kargil_district_and_Buddhists_which_erupted_in_1989_have_now_subsided._…_However,_even_the_Muslims_of_Jammu,_who_are_not_Kashmiri_speaking,_do_not_necessarily_support_the_demands_of_the_valley_Kashmiri_Muslims._…_Mistrust,_however,_remains_between_Muslims_and_the_displaced_Kashmiri_Pandits,_some_of_whom_are_now_demanding_a_separate_homeland_in_the_valley_for_the_700,000_Pandits_living_in_different_parts_of_India.") Spearheaded by a group seeking creation of an independent state based on demands for [self-determination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-determination "Self-determination"), the insurgency was taken over within the first few years of its outbreak by Pakistan-backed [Jihadist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihadism "Jihadism") groups striving for merger with Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEChowdhary2016pp._111–112:_"As_militancy_gained_ground,_there_was_mushrooming_of_militant_organisations_with_different_ideologies_and_different_objectives._While_India_remained_the_common_target_for_all_these_organisations,_there_were_lot_of_internal_differences._The_difference_was_not_merely_represented_by_the_ultimate_objectives_of_JKLF_(complete_independence_of_erstwhile_princely_state_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir_from_both_India_and_Pakistan-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEChowdhary2016pp._111–112:_"As_militancy_gained_ground,_there_was_mushrooming_of_militant_organisations_with_different_ideologies_and_different_objectives._While_India_remained_the_common_target_for_all_these_organisations,_there_were_lot_of_internal_differences._The_difference_was_not_merely_represented_by_the_ultimate_objectives_of_JKLF_(complete_independence_of_erstwhile_princely_state_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir_from_both_India_and_Pakistan)_and_Hizb_(merger_with_Pakistan)_but_also_with_regard_to_the_role_of_religion_in_the_movement._A_number_of_outfits_like_Allah_Tigers_were_keen_on_enforcing_%E2%80%98Islamic%E2%80%99_code_on_the_people_as_well._It_%E2%80%98went_about_smashing_Srinagar%E2%80%99s_bars,_closing_down_cinema_halls,_video_parlours_and_beauty_parlours,_saying_that_they_were_un-Islamic._It_was_decreed_that_all_women_would_wear_the_burqa,_and_dress_according_to_Islamic_tradition%E2%80%99_(Sidhva,_1992:_40%E2%80%932)._There_were_others_who_saw_armed_militancy_in_Kashmir_as_part_of_the_Pan-Islamic_struggle_being_waged_at_the_global_le)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEHussain2021(p._324-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEHussain2021(p._324)_%22Pakistani_support_gave_a_religious_tone_to_the_armed_insurgency_in_Kashmir,_overshadowing_the_nationalist_vision_of_an_independent_and_united_state_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir._..._Fearful_that_the_independent_ideology_of_the_JKLF_would_sideline_their_interests_in_the_Valley,_Pakistan_abandoned_the_JKLF_and_supported_militant_groups_that_would_advocate_Kashmir%E2%80%99s_accession_to_Pakistan.%22_(p._325)_%22After_the_intervention_of_Pakistan_in_Kashmir_post_1989,_the_Jamaat_saw_Kashmir_as_a_part_of_the_worldwide_Muslim_community,_and_its_incorporation_into_the_Muslim_state_of_Pakistan_as_the_first_step_toward_eventual_unity_of_all_Muslims._Thereafter,_the_party_provided_a_religious_rationale_for_advocating_Kashmir%E2%80%99s_accession_to_Pakistan,_and_defined_the_armed_struggle_against_India_as_a_holy_war%E2%80%94a_jihad._..._In_the_early_1990s,_the_Jamaat_took_center_stage_in_the_militant_movement,_and_its_armed_wing,_the_Hizbul_Mujahideen_(HM),_gave_the_jihad_a_practical_shape._This_powerful_group,_funde)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWarikoo2011p._78:_"During_the_first_phase_of_militancy_in_Kashmir_which_started_in_1989,_the_Islamist_militant_groups_strived_to_"bring_structural_changes_at_cultural_levels_of_Kashmir_society",_seeking_to_Islamicize_the_socio-political_set-up_in_the_Valley_to_bring_it_in_tune_with_the_Islamic_state_of_Pakistan_and_the_Muslim_Ummah._Though_militancy_in_Kashmir_was_launched_initially_by_the_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front_(JKLF-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWarikoo2011p._78:_"During_the_first_phase_of_militancy_in_Kashmir_which_started_in_1989,_the_Islamist_militant_groups_strived_to_"bring_structural_changes_at_cultural_levels_of_Kashmir_society",_seeking_to_Islamicize_the_socio-political_set-up_in_the_Valley_to_bring_it_in_tune_with_the_Islamic_state_of_Pakistan_and_the_Muslim_Ummah._Though_militancy_in_Kashmir_was_launched_initially_by_the_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front_(JKLF)_ostensibly_to_achieve_azadi_(independence),_within_a_few_months_a_number_of_militant_groups_emerged_advocating_Nizam-e-Mustafa_as_the_objective_of_their_struggle._Now_the_term_azadi_gave_way_to_jihad._Various_Islamist_groups_like_Jamaat-e-Islami_and_its_militant_wing_Hizbul_Mujahideen,_women%E2%80%99s_wing_Dukhtaran-e-Millat,_Jamiat-_ul-Mujahideen,_Allah_Tigers,_Jamiat-ul-Ulemma_Islam,_Al_Badr,_Al_Jihad_Force,_Al_Umar_Mujahideen,_Muslim_Mujahideen,_Islamic_Students_League,_Zia_Tigers_etc._proclaimed_the_objective_of_their_struggle_as_Islamicization_of_socio-political_and_e)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWebb2012p._44:_"The_first_wave_of_militancy_from_1988_through_to_1991_was_very_much_an_urban,_middle-class_affair_dominated_by_the_secular,_pro-independence_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front_(JKLF-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWebb2012p._44:_"The_first_wave_of_militancy_from_1988_through_to_1991_was_very_much_an_urban,_middle-class_affair_dominated_by_the_secular,_pro-independence_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front_(JKLF)_(Schofield_1996:_240)._Much_of_the_fighting_was_concentrated_in_Srinagar,_and_also_certain_rural_centers_such_as_Anantnag,_Baramulla_and_Kupwara,_while_most_of_the_militants_were_unemployed_university_graduates_who_had_campaigned_for_the_MUF_in_the_1987_election._..._Gradually_the_number_of_militant_groups_began_to_increase,_with_the_JKLF_losing_its_position_of_dominance_to_the_Islamist,_pro-Pakistan_Hizbul-Mujahideen_in_the_early_1990s_(Jones_2008;_Kumar_2002)._The_rise_of_Islamic,_pro-Pakistan_groups_is_frequently_associated_with_a_shift_to_a_more_rural-based_militancy_(Howard_1999:_40).%22-29)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mathur2016-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-Mathur2016) The militancy continued through the 1990s and early 2000s—by which time it was being driven largely by foreign militants[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEChowdhary2016p._112:_"According_to_[Sikand],_after_the_Mujahideen_victory_in_Afghanistan_in_1992,_‘numerous_jihadist_outfits_in_Pakistan_began_turning_their_attention_towards_Kashmir._By_the_late_1990s,_these_Pakistani_jihadists_were_playing_a_key_role_in_the_fighting_in_Kashmir,_eclipsing_even_local_Kashmiri_groups’_(Sikand,_2001:_222-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEChowdhary2016p._112:_"According_to_[Sikand],_after_the_Mujahideen_victory_in_Afghanistan_in_1992,_‘numerous_jihadist_outfits_in_Pakistan_began_turning_their_attention_towards_Kashmir._By_the_late_1990s,_these_Pakistani_jihadists_were_playing_a_key_role_in_the_fighting_in_Kashmir,_eclipsing_even_local_Kashmiri_groups’_(Sikand,_2001:_222)._Harkat-ul-Ansar,_Al-Faran,_Harkat-ul-Mujahideen_and_Jaish-e-Mohammed_were_such_organisations_that_dominated_the_scenario_of_militancy_at_different_points_in_time._The_increased_number_of_foreign_militants_in_the_period_after_mid-1990s_gets_reflected_from_the_large_percentage_of_the_killing_of_these_militants_by_Indian_security_forces_as_compared_to_the_local_militants_%E2%80%93_from_5.7_per_cent_foreign_militants_killed_in_1995,_the_percentage_was_increased_to_53.9_per_cent_in_2000_and_69.38_per_cent_in_2003_(Routray,_2012:_182).%22-31)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBehera2006155:_"With_the_Hazratbal_siege_and_surrender_of_JKLF_militants_in_April_1993,_the_insurgency_took_a_new_course._It_became_increasingly_difficult_for_the_Hizb-ul-Mujahideen_to_recruit_members_of_the_Kashmiri_cadre._Attributing_this_to_fatigue,_Pakistan_decided_to_push_more_Afghan_veterans,_Pakistani_nationals,_and_foreign_mercenaries_into_the_Valley._This_trend_gathered_momentum_in_1996,_when_the_Taliban_marched_into_Kabul."-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBehera2006155:_"With_the_Hazratbal_siege_and_surrender_of_JKLF_militants_in_April_1993,_the_insurgency_took_a_new_course._It_became_increasingly_difficult_for_the_Hizb-ul-Mujahideen_to_recruit_members_of_the_Kashmiri_cadre._Attributing_this_to_fatigue,_Pakistan_decided_to_push_more_Afghan_veterans,_Pakistani_nationals,_and_foreign_mercenaries_into_the_Valley._This_trend_gathered_momentum_in_1996,_when_the_Taliban_marched_into_Kabul.") and spread to parts of the adjoining Jammu region[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKumarPuri2009p._268:_"By_the_end_of_the_1990s,_there_were_more_Pakistanis_than_Kashmiris_amongst_the_mujahideen._The_Lashkar’s_list_of_‘martyred_commanders’,_for_example,_named_men_from_all_over_Pakistan._The_mujahideen_had_more_sophisticated_arms,_communications_and_planning,_and_they_inflicted_much_greater_damage_in_raids_on_army_and_police_posts,_convoys_and_barracks,_government_buildings_and_civilians._Within_Kashmir,_conflict_spread_from_the_Kashmir_valley_to_the_Muslim_majority_districts_of_Jammu,_where_Hindus_and_nomads_began_to_be_targeted_in_the_border_villages."-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKumarPuri2009p._268:_"By_the_end_of_the_1990s,_there_were_more_Pakistanis_than_Kashmiris_amongst_the_mujahideen._The_Lashkar’s_list_of_‘martyred_commanders’,_for_example,_named_men_from_all_over_Pakistan._The_mujahideen_had_more_sophisticated_arms,_communications_and_planning,_and_they_inflicted_much_greater_damage_in_raids_on_army_and_police_posts,_convoys_and_barracks,_government_buildings_and_civilians._Within_Kashmir,_conflict_spread_from_the_Kashmir_valley_to_the_Muslim_majority_districts_of_Jammu,_where_Hindus_and_nomads_began_to_be_targeted_in_the_border_villages.")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBhatia2020p._8:_"Insurgency_originated_in_Kashmir_as_an_indigenous_secessionist_movement._However,_by_the_time_it_spread_widely_to_various_parts_of_Jammu,_it_had_turned_extremist_and_ruthlessly_violent_in_character_due_to_the_involvement_of_non-Kashmiri_militants_sneaking_in_from_across_the_India–Pakistan_borders._For_around_a_decade,_insurgency_was_at_its_peak_in_various_parts_of_Jammu,_resulting_in_public_killings_due_to_frequent_incidents_of_blasts_and_attacks_by_militants_in_and_around_Jammu_city."-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBhatia2020p._8:_"Insurgency_originated_in_Kashmir_as_an_indigenous_secessionist_movement._However,_by_the_time_it_spread_widely_to_various_parts_of_Jammu,_it_had_turned_extremist_and_ruthlessly_violent_in_character_due_to_the_involvement_of_non-Kashmiri_militants_sneaking_in_from_across_the_India–Pakistan_borders._For_around_a_decade,_insurgency_was_at_its_peak_in_various_parts_of_Jammu,_resulting_in_public_killings_due_to_frequent_incidents_of_blasts_and_attacks_by_militants_in_and_around_Jammu_city.")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBose2021p._100:_"With_the_help_of_the_renegades,_the_Indian_forces_were_able_to_reassert_control_over_most_of_the_Kashmir_Valley._Guerrilla_activity_moved_out_to_remote,_forested_parts_of_the_Valley,_and_in_the_late_1990s_a_new,_deadly_theatre_of_insurgency_opened_up_in_the_Jammu_region’s_Rajouri_and_Poonch_districts_(on_which_more_below-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBose2021p._100:_"With_the_help_of_the_renegades,_the_Indian_forces_were_able_to_reassert_control_over_most_of_the_Kashmir_Valley._Guerrilla_activity_moved_out_to_remote,_forested_parts_of_the_Valley,_and_in_the_late_1990s_a_new,_deadly_theatre_of_insurgency_opened_up_in_the_Jammu_region’s_Rajouri_and_Poonch_districts_(on_which_more_below),_in_addition_to_the_Doda-Kishtwar_zone.%22-35)—but declined thereafter. The fighting resulted in tens of thousands of casualties, both combatant and civilian. The militancy also resulted in the [exodus of Kashmiri Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exodus_of_Kashmiri_Hindus "Exodus of Kashmiri Hindus") from the predominantly Muslim Kashmir Valley in the early 1990s. Counterinsurgency by the Indian government was coupled with repression of the local population and increased militarisation of the region, while various insurgent groups engaged in a variety of criminal activity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2006p._273–274:_"Upwards_of_100,000_of_[Kashmiri_Hindus]_left_the_state_during_the_early_1990s;_their_cause_was_quickly_taken_up_by_the_Hindu_right._As_the_government_sought_to_locate_‘suspects’_and_weed_out_Pakistani_‘infiltrators’,_the_entire_population_was_subjected_to_a_fierce_repression._By_the_end_of_the_1990s,_the_Indian_military_presence_had_escalated_to_approximately_one_soldier_or_paramilitary_policeman_for_every_five_Kashmiris,_and_some_30,000_people_had_died_in_the_conflict._Subsequent_years_saw_a_reduction_in_violence_coupled_with_widespread_participation_in_Indian_elections,_and_a_consequent_lowering_of_troop_strength_despite_the_absence_of_a_settlement._The_general_consensus_is_that_the_Kashmiris_seek_a_degree_of_regional_autonomy,_not_a_union_with_Pakistan."-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2006p._273–274:_"Upwards_of_100,000_of_[Kashmiri_Hindus]_left_the_state_during_the_early_1990s;_their_cause_was_quickly_taken_up_by_the_Hindu_right._As_the_government_sought_to_locate_‘suspects’_and_weed_out_Pakistani_‘infiltrators’,_the_entire_population_was_subjected_to_a_fierce_repression._By_the_end_of_the_1990s,_the_Indian_military_presence_had_escalated_to_approximately_one_soldier_or_paramilitary_policeman_for_every_five_Kashmiris,_and_some_30,000_people_had_died_in_the_conflict._Subsequent_years_saw_a_reduction_in_violence_coupled_with_widespread_participation_in_Indian_elections,_and_a_consequent_lowering_of_troop_strength_despite_the_absence_of_a_settlement._The_general_consensus_is_that_the_Kashmiris_seek_a_degree_of_regional_autonomy,_not_a_union_with_Pakistan.")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWebb2012p._45:_"The_Islamization_and_internationalization_of_militancy_in_Kashmir_was_also_accompanied_by_its_criminalization_(Schofield_1996:_267–8-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWebb2012p._45:_"The_Islamization_and_internationalization_of_militancy_in_Kashmir_was_also_accompanied_by_its_criminalization_(Schofield_1996:_267–8)._Initially_the_militants_enjoyed_wide_popular_support;_however,_as_people_became_increasingly_war-weary_from_the_steadily_mounting_number_of_casualties,_this_support_gradually_began_to_evaporate._Consequently,_the_militants_were_forced_to_resort_to_tactics_such_as_kidnapping_to_extort_money_and_food_from_the_local_population_(Ganguly_1997:_139;_Baweja_1995:_19%E2%80%9325)._This,_however,_only_further_alienated_the_militants_from_common_Kashmiris_and_initiated_a_vicious_cycle_of_increasing_resentment_and_violence_perpetrated_both_by_the_militants_and_Indian_security_forces,_who_increasingly_engaged_in_torture,_rape_and_extra-judicial_killings_(Amnesty_International_1995;_Asia_Watch_and_Physicians_for_Human_Rights_1993)._Indian_apologists_claim_that_such_allegations_are_usually_false_or_grossly_exaggerated_and_point_out_that_the_militants_themselves_are_)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKumarPuri2009p._268:_"The_Indian_government_adopted_increasingly_draconian_measures_in_response,_and_civilians_were_frequently_trapped_in_the_battle_between_Indian_troops_and_the_Islamic_militias._The_counter-insurgency_policy_of_using_erstwhile_mujahideen_to_fight_present_ones_worsened_an_already_fragile_law_and_order_infrastructure,_letting_in_revenge_killings._By_the_end_of_the_decade,_more_than_35,000_people_had_been_killed,_the_vast_majority_Muslim,_and_families_who_had_lost_one_member_at_the_hands_of_Islamic_militias_and_another_at_the_hands_of_the_security_forces_were_more_a_norm_than_exceptions_in_the_Kashmir_valley."-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKumarPuri2009p._268:_"The_Indian_government_adopted_increasingly_draconian_measures_in_response,_and_civilians_were_frequently_trapped_in_the_battle_between_Indian_troops_and_the_Islamic_militias._The_counter-insurgency_policy_of_using_erstwhile_mujahideen_to_fight_present_ones_worsened_an_already_fragile_law_and_order_infrastructure,_letting_in_revenge_killings._By_the_end_of_the_decade,_more_than_35,000_people_had_been_killed,_the_vast_majority_Muslim,_and_families_who_had_lost_one_member_at_the_hands_of_Islamic_militias_and_another_at_the_hands_of_the_security_forces_were_more_a_norm_than_exceptions_in_the_Kashmir_valley.") The 2010s were marked by civil unrest within the Kashmir Valley, fuelled by unyielding militarisation, rights violations, mis-rule and corruption,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEZutshi2019p._137:_"The_lack_of_resolution_of_the_basic_issues_related_to_the_insurgency_and_its_impact,_especially_the_continued_presence_and_impunity_of_the_Indian_security_forces_in_the_Valley,_blatant_human_rights_violations_within_and_outside_the_Valley,_combined_with_rampant_mis-governance,_corruption,_and_the_inability_of_state_governments_to_provide_basic_services,_has_provided_ample_cause_for_young_Kashmiri_Muslim_men_and_women_to_participate_in_the_azaadi_movement."-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEZutshi2019p._137:_"The_lack_of_resolution_of_the_basic_issues_related_to_the_insurgency_and_its_impact,_especially_the_continued_presence_and_impunity_of_the_Indian_security_forces_in_the_Valley,_blatant_human_rights_violations_within_and_outside_the_Valley,_combined_with_rampant_mis-governance,_corruption,_and_the_inability_of_state_governments_to_provide_basic_services,_has_provided_ample_cause_for_young_Kashmiri_Muslim_men_and_women_to_participate_in_the_azaadi_movement.")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBose2021pp._131–132:_"That_new_phase_of_the_Kashmir_conflict_came_to_be_symbolised_not_by_the_gun-wielding_insurgent_–_armed_militancy_did_not_revive_significantly_–_but_by_the_stone-pelter._Mass_stone-pelting_at_the_enforcers_of_the_state-of-exception_regime_revived_a_decades-old_tradition_of_protest_in_the_Valley,_which_had_been_temporarily_displaced_by_the_Kalashnikov-carrying_insurgents_from_1990_to_the_mid-2000s._Major_stone-pelting_uprisings_led_by_a_new_generation_of_youth_born_in_the_1990s_broke_out_in_the_Kashmir_Valley_in_2010_and_again_in_2016,_and_during_the_decade_the_stone_replaced_the_AK-47s_wielded_by_the_previous_generation_as_the_weapon_of_everyday_struggle."-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBose2021pp._131–132:_"That_new_phase_of_the_Kashmir_conflict_came_to_be_symbolised_not_by_the_gun-wielding_insurgent_–_armed_militancy_did_not_revive_significantly_–_but_by_the_stone-pelter._Mass_stone-pelting_at_the_enforcers_of_the_state-of-exception_regime_revived_a_decades-old_tradition_of_protest_in_the_Valley,_which_had_been_temporarily_displaced_by_the_Kalashnikov-carrying_insurgents_from_1990_to_the_mid-2000s._Major_stone-pelting_uprisings_led_by_a_new_generation_of_youth_born_in_the_1990s_broke_out_in_the_Kashmir_Valley_in_2010_and_again_in_2016,_and_during_the_decade_the_stone_replaced_the_AK-47s_wielded_by_the_previous_generation_as_the_weapon_of_everyday_struggle.") wherein protesting local youths violently clashed with Indian security forces,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWebb2012p._49:_"Since_mid-2010,_Srinagar_and_other_areas_of_the_Valley_have_been_regularly_shut_down_by_violent_protests,_strikes_and_curfews,_as_a_new_generation_of_Kashmiris_who_have_grown_up_surrounded_by_political_violence_continue_to_press_the_claim_for_separation_from_India."-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWebb2012p._49:_"Since_mid-2010,_Srinagar_and_other_areas_of_the_Valley_have_been_regularly_shut_down_by_violent_protests,_strikes_and_curfews,_as_a_new_generation_of_Kashmiris_who_have_grown_up_surrounded_by_political_violence_continue_to_press_the_claim_for_separation_from_India.") with large-scale demonstrations taking place during the [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Kashmir_unrest "2010 Kashmir unrest")[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[note 2\]](#refnote-44) and [2016 unrests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Kashmir_unrest "2016 Kashmir unrest").[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[note 3\]](#refnote-48) Further unrest in the region erupted after the [2019 Pulwama attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Pulwama_attack "2019 Pulwama attack").[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-49)

Over the course of the long conflict, and especially since the outbreak of insurgency, India and Pakistan have been accused of violating human rights in the Kashmir region, while insurgents and militants have committed many atrocities in the region. Indian security forces, including [local police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_police "Jammu and Kashmir police") from the region, have committed serious excesses during counterinsurgency in the Kashmir Valley, which include [human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir "Human rights abuses in Jammu and Kashmir") such as extrajudicial killings, arson, torture, unlawful detentions, [rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_during_the_Kashmir_conflict "Rape during the Kashmir conflict") and [enforced disappearances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enforced_disappearance#India "Enforced disappearance"), while the government has also caused political repression in the region.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWebb2012p._139:_"Since_the_beginning_of_the_current_uprising_in_1989,_numerous_reports_(e.g._Amnesty_International_1995;_Asia_Watch_and_Physicians_for_Human_Rights_1993-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWebb2012p._139:_"Since_the_beginning_of_the_current_uprising_in_1989,_numerous_reports_(e.g._Amnesty_International_1995;_Asia_Watch_and_Physicians_for_Human_Rights_1993)_have_detailed_the_human_rights_violations_perpetrated_by_security_forces_in_Kashmir_against_militants,_peaceful_protesters_and_ordinary_civilians_alike._Arbitrary_arrest;_beatings;_electrocution;_the_use_of_a_large_roller_to_break_victims'_bones,_causing_multiple_organ_failure;_intentional_destruction_of_property;_rape;_execution_and_other_heinous_acts_have_been_perpetrated_against_Kashmiris_with_regularity_since_1989.%22_(p._141):_%22Furthermore,_many_of_the_atrocities_committed_in_Kashmir_since_1989_have_been_perpetrated_by_organizations_indigenous_to_the_state,_including_the_Special_Operations_Group_of_the_Jammu_and_Kashmir_police_that_have_widely_been_accused_of_human_rights_abuses_and_operating_%22above_the_law%22_(Amnesty_International_2011:_28,_Amnesty_International_2001).%22-50)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEZutshi2019p._144:_"The_Indian_government_suspended_the_regional_government,_imposed_president’s_rule_over_the_state,_and_brought_the_full_might_of_its_military_and_border_security_forces_to_bear_on_quelling_the_insurgency._As_the_insurgency_came_to_be_increasingly_hijacked_by_outside_forces_by_the_mid-_to_late_1990s,_with_Pakistani-supported_organizations_such_as_Harkat-ul-Mujahideen,_Jaish-e-Muhammad,_and_Lashkar-i-Taiba_seeking_to_use_this_as_an_opportunity_to_destabilize_India_(Chowdhary_2002:_2398–9-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEZutshi2019p._144:_"The_Indian_government_suspended_the_regional_government,_imposed_president’s_rule_over_the_state,_and_brought_the_full_might_of_its_military_and_border_security_forces_to_bear_on_quelling_the_insurgency._As_the_insurgency_came_to_be_increasingly_hijacked_by_outside_forces_by_the_mid-_to_late_1990s,_with_Pakistani-supported_organizations_such_as_Harkat-ul-Mujahideen,_Jaish-e-Muhammad,_and_Lashkar-i-Taiba_seeking_to_use_this_as_an_opportunity_to_destabilize_India_(Chowdhary_2002:_2398–9),_India_ratcheted_up_its_military_response_accordingly._It_made_no_distinction_between_the_homegrown_indigenous_revolt_and_the_external_forces_using_it_as_a_means_to_further_their_own_agenda_of_global_jihad._Search_and_seizure_operations,_disappearances_(especially_of_young_men),_torture,_arson,_murder,_and_rape_by_Indian_security_forces_became_the_order_of_the_day.%22-51) Pakistan has committed [abuses in Pakistan-administered Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses_in_Azad_Kashmir "Human rights abuses in Azad Kashmir"), including [enforced disappearances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enforced_disappearances_in_Pakistan "Enforced disappearances in Pakistan"), murder, torture, unlawful detentions and political repression,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBalcerowiczKuszewska2022p._135:_"The_population_of_Pakistani-administered_Jammu_and_Kashmir_(PaJK-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBalcerowiczKuszewska2022p._135:_"The_population_of_Pakistani-administered_Jammu_and_Kashmir_(PaJK)_falls_victim_to_a_range_of_abuses,_including_what_can_be_called_a_four-pack_of_enforced_disappearances,_illegal_detentions,_torture_and_extra-judicial_killings._It_is_the_constitutional/legal_provisions_''imposed''_on_Pakistani_administered_Kashmir_by_the_Pakistani_authorities_that_makes_such_violations_possible.%22-52) and also tacitly [permits and sponsors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_and_state-sponsored_terrorism "Pakistan and state-sponsored terrorism") operations of [Islamist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamist "Islamist") militant groups there, many of which it created, funds and arms.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESnedden2021pp.274–275:_"During_the_early_years_of_the_insurgency,_Pakistan_also_overcame_its_early_surprise_about_the_Kashmiris’_serious_anti-India_actions_and_quickly_began_to_house,_train,_arm,_support_and_indoctrinate_various_anti-India_militants._Initially,_these_militants_comprised_Muslim_Kashmiris,_with_the_JKLF_the_major,_and_then_pre-eminent,_group_that_Pakistan’s_security_forces_first_supported._Much_of_this_Pakistani_assistance_was_provided_at_locations_in_Pakistan-Administered_Azad_Kashmir,_whose_leaders_had_long_considered_their_region_to_be_the_‘base_camp’_for_the_liberation_of_Indian_J&K,_or_in_Pakistan’s_adjacent_North-West_Frontier_Province_(renamed_Khyber_Pakhtunkhwa_Province_in_2010-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESnedden2021pp.274–275:_"During_the_early_years_of_the_insurgency,_Pakistan_also_overcame_its_early_surprise_about_the_Kashmiris’_serious_anti-India_actions_and_quickly_began_to_house,_train,_arm,_support_and_indoctrinate_various_anti-India_militants._Initially,_these_militants_comprised_Muslim_Kashmiris,_with_the_JKLF_the_major,_and_then_pre-eminent,_group_that_Pakistan’s_security_forces_first_supported._Much_of_this_Pakistani_assistance_was_provided_at_locations_in_Pakistan-Administered_Azad_Kashmir,_whose_leaders_had_long_considered_their_region_to_be_the_‘base_camp’_for_the_liberation_of_Indian_J&K,_or_in_Pakistan’s_adjacent_North-West_Frontier_Province_(renamed_Khyber_Pakhtunkhwa_Province_in_2010)._..._Alongside_the_JKLF%E2%80%99s_debasement_in_the_early_1990s,_Pakistan%E2%80%99s_security_forces,_led_by_the_powerful_and_secretive_ISI,_only_provided_assistance_to_militant_groups_that_wanted_to_join_J&K_with_Pakistan._With_Pakistan%E2%80%99s_support_and_guidance,_these_pro-Pakistan_militants_slowly_coalesced_into)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGiustozzi2023pp._19–20:_"Already_in_1984_plans_started_reportedly_being_drafted_in_Pakistan,_following_a_direct_order_of_President/dictator_Zia_ul_Haq,_for_sponsoring_an_insurgency_in_Kashmir._Soon_Kashmiri_separatists_were_being_trained_in_the_camps_of_Jama’at_e_Islami,_the_main_Pakistani_Islamist_party_and_a_close_ally_of_Zia._..._By_the_mid-1980s,_the_Pakistanis_were_already_providing_support_to_the_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front_(JKLF-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGiustozzi2023pp._19–20:_"Already_in_1984_plans_started_reportedly_being_drafted_in_Pakistan,_following_a_direct_order_of_President/dictator_Zia_ul_Haq,_for_sponsoring_an_insurgency_in_Kashmir._Soon_Kashmiri_separatists_were_being_trained_in_the_camps_of_Jama’at_e_Islami,_the_main_Pakistani_Islamist_party_and_a_close_ally_of_Zia._..._By_the_mid-1980s,_the_Pakistanis_were_already_providing_support_to_the_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front_(JKLF)._The_Kashmir_operation_rapidly_escalated_to_the_point_when_in_1990_'several_hundred_Kashmiris_per_month_were_crossing_into_Pakistani_territory_for_military_training_and_then_returning_to_Kashmir_to_fight_the_Indian_occupation'.The_nationalist_tendencies_of_the_JKLF_were_not_to_Pakistan%E2%80%99s_liking._For_this_reason_the_Pakistani_ISI_abandoned_the_JKLF_and_started_supporting_other_groups,_primarily_Hizb_ul_Mujahideen_(HM),_an_Islamist_group_linked_to_Jama%E2%80%99at_e_Islami_(JeI),_which,_contrary_to_the_JKLF_advocated_the_accession_of_Kashmir_to_Pakistan._But_even)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGatesRoy2014pp._100–101:_"In_the_mid-1980s,_the_Pakistan_Army_started_giving_active_support_to_the_''tanzeems''._Instead_of_directly_recruiting_and_arming_the_tribal_raiders_as_the_Pakistan_Army_had_done_during_1947_and_in_1965,_the_militant_organizations_were_provided_with_arms_and_money._..._Since_the_late_1980s,_the_Inter_Services_Intelligence_(ISI-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGatesRoy2014pp._100–101:_"In_the_mid-1980s,_the_Pakistan_Army_started_giving_active_support_to_the_''tanzeems''._Instead_of_directly_recruiting_and_arming_the_tribal_raiders_as_the_Pakistan_Army_had_done_during_1947_and_in_1965,_the_militant_organizations_were_provided_with_arms_and_money._..._Since_the_late_1980s,_the_Inter_Services_Intelligence_(ISI)_of_Pakistan_became_the_principal_instrument_for_funding_and_training_insurgents._In_1993,_the_ISI_spent_US$_3.3_million_per_month_on_the_Kashmiri_militants_(Talbot_2002:_313)._In_1992,_the_ISI_operated_13_permanent,_18_temporary_and_8_joint_military_training_camps_for_the_Kashmiri_youths._About_3,800_Kashmiri_militants_were_trained_in_these_camps._By_1993,_some_20,000_young_Kashmiris_were_trained_and_equipped_by_the_ISI_ready_to_be_pushed_into_Kashmir_(Mir_2006:_5)._..._Thanks_to_the_ISI,_the_divisional_and_area_commanders_of_the_insurgents_were_given_satellite_phones_to_maintain_communications_not_only_with_their_Pakistani_sponsors_across_the) Insurgents and militants have committed killings and [assassinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassinations "Assassinations") of civilians, including several [massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacres "Massacres") of religious and ethnic minorities, especially Hindus in both Jammu region and the Kashmir Valley, whom they have also driven out of the region or displaced within it, as well as abductions, arson, [rapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_during_the_Kashmir_conflict#Militants "Rape during the Kashmir conflict") and [terrorist attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorist_attacks_in_India "Terrorist attacks in India"), and [forced an Islamic code of conduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_radicalism "Islamic radicalism") on the larger society.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEByman2005p._167:_"Human_Rights_Watch_also_reports_that_militants_used_violence_to_drive_Hindus_out_of_the_state_of_[Jammu_and]_Kashmir._They_massacred_Hindu_villagers,_assassinated_Hindu_leaders,_destroyed_Hindu_temples,_and_otherwise_threatened_Hindus_with_violence._Thousands_of_Hindus_fled_the_Valley_or_were_displaced_within_Kashmir."-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEByman2005p._167:_"Human_Rights_Watch_also_reports_that_militants_used_violence_to_drive_Hindus_out_of_the_state_of_[Jammu_and]_Kashmir._They_massacred_Hindu_villagers,_assassinated_Hindu_leaders,_destroyed_Hindu_temples,_and_otherwise_threatened_Hindus_with_violence._Thousands_of_Hindus_fled_the_Valley_or_were_displaced_within_Kashmir.")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEZutshi2019pp._132–133:_"Violence_engulfed_the_Valley,_with_targeted_assassinations_and_kidnappings_of_important_political_and_academic_figures_by_Kashmiri_insurgents;_bombings_and_other_attacks_by_external_groups;_thefts_and_killings_by_local_criminal_gangs;_and_reprisals_by_Indian_security_forces._Kashmir_became_inhabitable_for_most_Kashmiris_as_any_semblance_of_community,_civil_society,_and_normalcy_was_replaced_by_terror,_lawlessness,_rumour,_and_suspicion._Many_Kashmiris_were_forced_to_leave_the_Valley_and_the_mass_exodus_in_the_first_few_years_of_the_insurgency_of_Kashmiri_Pandits,_the_minority_community_of_Hindus—which_felt_increasingly_targeted_by_local_and_external_groups—further_polarized_Kashmiri_society,_this_time_along_lines_of_religion._These_developments_subverted_the_popular_nature_of_the_insurgency,_tarnishing_the_very_real_political_grievances_that_underlay_it_with_the_brush_of_criminality_and_Islamic_radicalism."-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEZutshi2019pp._132–133:_"Violence_engulfed_the_Valley,_with_targeted_assassinations_and_kidnappings_of_important_political_and_academic_figures_by_Kashmiri_insurgents;_bombings_and_other_attacks_by_external_groups;_thefts_and_killings_by_local_criminal_gangs;_and_reprisals_by_Indian_security_forces._Kashmir_became_inhabitable_for_most_Kashmiris_as_any_semblance_of_community,_civil_society,_and_normalcy_was_replaced_by_terror,_lawlessness,_rumour,_and_suspicion._Many_Kashmiris_were_forced_to_leave_the_Valley_and_the_mass_exodus_in_the_first_few_years_of_the_insurgency_of_Kashmiri_Pandits,_the_minority_community_of_Hindus—which_felt_increasingly_targeted_by_local_and_external_groups—further_polarized_Kashmiri_society,_this_time_along_lines_of_religion._These_developments_subverted_the_popular_nature_of_the_insurgency,_tarnishing_the_very_real_political_grievances_that_underlay_it_with_the_brush_of_criminality_and_Islamic_radicalism.")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBhatia2021p._84:_"That_being_so,_when_Kashmir-based_insurgency_spread_to_parts_of_Jammu_in_the_late_1990s_and_early_2000,_many_Muslim_youth_of_these_districts_joined_insurgency_alongside_Kashmiris_and_many_extremist_Pakistan-backed_groups._Insurgency,_thus,_took_a_brutal_shape_when_it_hit_these_regions_and_many_communal_killings_have_been_recorded_during_those_periods._Hindus_were_targeted_and_killed_in_a_few_villages,_during_marriage_ceremonies_and_while_travelling_in_buses_(Swami,_1998,_Puri,_2008-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBhatia2021p._84:_"That_being_so,_when_Kashmir-based_insurgency_spread_to_parts_of_Jammu_in_the_late_1990s_and_early_2000,_many_Muslim_youth_of_these_districts_joined_insurgency_alongside_Kashmiris_and_many_extremist_Pakistan-backed_groups._Insurgency,_thus,_took_a_brutal_shape_when_it_hit_these_regions_and_many_communal_killings_have_been_recorded_during_those_periods._Hindus_were_targeted_and_killed_in_a_few_villages,_during_marriage_ceremonies_and_while_travelling_in_buses_(Swami,_1998,_Puri,_2008)._In_these_districts,_the_responses_of_the_Hindu_communities_was_also_extreme,_as_many_vehemently_endorsed_the_right-wing_politics.%22-58)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEChowdhary2016p._115:_"Asia_Watch_and_Physicians_for_Human_Rights_noted_as_early_as_1992_the_involvement_of_militants_in_incidents_of_abduction_and_execution_of_civilians_suspected_to_be_government_informers_or_supporting_the_Government_of_India,_and_raping_women_and_placing_various_social_restrictions_on_them_(Asia_Watch_Committee_et_al.,_1993:_142–3-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEChowdhary2016p._115:_"Asia_Watch_and_Physicians_for_Human_Rights_noted_as_early_as_1992_the_involvement_of_militants_in_incidents_of_abduction_and_execution_of_civilians_suspected_to_be_government_informers_or_supporting_the_Government_of_India,_and_raping_women_and_placing_various_social_restrictions_on_them_(Asia_Watch_Committee_et_al.,_1993:_142–3)._Also_there_was_lack_of_tolerance_for_political_dissent_within_the_separatist_politics._During_the_post-1990_period_many_people_were_killed_by_the_armed_militants_because_of_their_ideological_position._..._Islamic_radicalisation_also_had_its_implications_for_the_militancy._Many_of_those_who_had_supported_the_militancy_in_the_initial_period,_purely_for_political_reasons,_started_getting_disillusioned_by_it_as_the_fundamentalist_elements_in_the_fringes_started_using_the_pro-militancy_environment_to_invoke_religious_and_moral_codes_as_well_as_to_assert_the_religious_dimensions_of_the_politics_of_''azadi''.%22-59) Multiple [wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_wars_and_conflicts "Indo-Pakistani wars and conflicts") and cross-border shelling between India and Pakistan along the LOC, at times aimed to enable cross-border movement of militants by Pakistan, has also caused civilian deaths and displacement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESnedden2021p._276:_"As_they_had_done_intermittently_since_1947,_the_armies_of_both_nations_engaged_in_bitter_artillery_and_small_arms_'duels',_or_exchanges,_along,_and_across,_either_the_heavy-militarised_LOC_in_J&K_or_the_more_southerly_border_between_Pakistani_Punjab_and_Indian_J&K’s_Jammu_region_(and_which_Pakistan_calls_the_'working_boundary',_as_it_considers_the_Kashmir_dispute_to_be_unresolved-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESnedden2021p._276:_"As_they_had_done_intermittently_since_1947,_the_armies_of_both_nations_engaged_in_bitter_artillery_and_small_arms_'duels',_or_exchanges,_along,_and_across,_either_the_heavy-militarised_LOC_in_J&K_or_the_more_southerly_border_between_Pakistani_Punjab_and_Indian_J&K’s_Jammu_region_(and_which_Pakistan_calls_the_'working_boundary',_as_it_considers_the_Kashmir_dispute_to_be_unresolved)._For_J&K-ites_living_nearby,_these_exchanges_have_invariably_resulted_in_significant_dislocation_and_damage_to_them,_their_dwellings_and_livestock._For_Pakistan,_one_aim_or_benefit_of_these_exchanges_was_to_create_diversions_to_enable_the_'exfiltration_or_infiltration'_of_militants_across_the_LOC.%22-60)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBhatia2020p._70:_"Due_to_the_de_facto,_unsettled_nature_of_the_LOC_as_well_as_the_frequent_India–Pakistan_military_skirmishes_and_their_sheer_intensity,_people_living_in_these_districts_are_almost_incessantly_caught_up_in_situations_of_instability._The_process_of_constructing_and_contesting_the_LOC_has_led_to_four_major_wars_(1947–1948,_1965,_1971_and_1999-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBhatia2020p._70:_"Due_to_the_de_facto,_unsettled_nature_of_the_LOC_as_well_as_the_frequent_India–Pakistan_military_skirmishes_and_their_sheer_intensity,_people_living_in_these_districts_are_almost_incessantly_caught_up_in_situations_of_instability._The_process_of_constructing_and_contesting_the_LOC_has_led_to_four_major_wars_(1947–1948,_1965,_1971_and_1999)_between_the_two_countries_with_enormous_implications_for_the_people_living_in_these_areas._Besides_economic_consequences_and_human_casualties,_multiple_displacements_across_the_LOC_have_led_to_the_phenomenon_of_'divided_families'._Almost_every_war_%E2%80%93_the_1947_and_1965_in_particular_%E2%80%93_led_to_the_migration_of_a_large_number_of_people_across_the_border,_some_of_whom_returned_after_a_gap_of_few_years._Even_during_times_of_relative_'normalcy',_these_areas_remain_prone_to_military_violence,_intermittent_firing,_shelling_and_displacements.%22-61) Indian security forces enjoy impunity for their actions and victims of their abuse lack legal redressal, with legislation preventing prosecution for abuses, and the government and a compliant judiciary enabling continued violations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWebb2012p._139:_"Additionally,_a_number_of_recent_reports_(e.g._Amnesty_International_2011,_Amnesty_International_2008-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWebb2012p._139:_"Additionally,_a_number_of_recent_reports_(e.g._Amnesty_International_2011,_Amnesty_International_2008)_have_highlighted_the_continuing_use_of_heavy-handed_tactics_by_security_forces_and_the_lack_of_legal_redress_available_to_victims_of_these_abuses.%22-62)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEHussain2021p._333:_"A_report_published_by_the_International_People’s_Tribunal_for_Human_Rights_and_Justice_in_Indian_administered_Kashmir_in_2012_provides_a_detailed_exposition_of_the_entrenched_culture_of_impunity_in_the_militarized_Valley._It_reveals_the_judicial_branch_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir_has_rendered_itself_subservient_to_the_power_of_the_executive_and_thus_condoned_continuous_human_rights_violations."-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEHussain2021p._333:_"A_report_published_by_the_International_People’s_Tribunal_for_Human_Rights_and_Justice_in_Indian_administered_Kashmir_in_2012_provides_a_detailed_exposition_of_the_entrenched_culture_of_impunity_in_the_militarized_Valley._It_reveals_the_judicial_branch_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir_has_rendered_itself_subservient_to_the_power_of_the_executive_and_thus_condoned_continuous_human_rights_violations.")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBalcerowiczKuszewska2022pp._37–38:_"In_certain_cases,_various_laws_functioning_in_Kashmir_provide_legal_shield_for_the_state-supported_policies_that_go_beyond_the_struggle_with_actual_terrorism-related_threats._The_Unlawful_Activities_(Prevention-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBalcerowiczKuszewska2022pp._37–38:_"In_certain_cases,_various_laws_functioning_in_Kashmir_provide_legal_shield_for_the_state-supported_policies_that_go_beyond_the_struggle_with_actual_terrorism-related_threats._The_Unlawful_Activities_(Prevention)_Act_(UAPA),_1967,_is_India%E2%80%99s_main_anti-terrorism_law,_subjected_to_several_amendments._Currently,_an_organisation_and_an_individual_can_be_pronounced_%E2%80%98terrorist%E2%80%99_if_they_endanger_India%E2%80%99s_sovereignty_and_integrity_by_%E2%80%98taking_part,_committing,_advocating,_abetting,_advising_or_inciting_the_commission_of,_any_unlawful_activity%E2%80%99._Anyone_deemed_capable_of_such_activities_can_be_arrested_for_six_months_without_trial_or_bail._Apart_from_preventing_real_terrorist_activities,_the_UAPA_serves_as_authorisation_of_oppression,_which_raises_concerns_of_HR_defenders._..._The_exemption_from_punishment_provided_for_those_who_bear_responsibility_for_HR_abuses_is_legitimised_and_remains_affiliated_within_the_framework_of_official_political,_economic,_sociocultural_an) Owing to tight controls on access by Pakistan, human rights violations in Pakistan-administered Kashmir remain underdocumented unlike those on the Indian side.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBalcerowiczKuszewska2022pp._117–120:_"Unlike_in_IaJK,_there_has_been_extremely_limited_access_to_information_that_would_allow_both_local_HR_activists_and_monitors_or_foreign_observers_and_researchers_to_freely_collect_more_detailed_data_of_statistical_importance._Most_such_attempts_have_to_be_undertaken_in_collusion_with_Pakistani_security_authorities_in_the_sense_that_the_latter_consistently_monitor_the_freedom_of_movement_of_researchers,_their_contacts,_duration_of_conversations_with_informers_etc.,_and_in_consequence_the_reports,_surveys_etc._which_have_so_far_been_published_are_rather_a_result_of_what_Pakistani_security_and_administrations_have_knowingly_approved,_at_least_to_some_extent._The_mere_legalisation_and_basic_activities_of_organisations_comparable_to_those_which_have_operated_in_IaJK_(such_as_JKCCS,_APDP,_IPTK-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBalcerowiczKuszewska2022pp._117–120:_"Unlike_in_IaJK,_there_has_been_extremely_limited_access_to_information_that_would_allow_both_local_HR_activists_and_monitors_or_foreign_observers_and_researchers_to_freely_collect_more_detailed_data_of_statistical_importance._Most_such_attempts_have_to_be_undertaken_in_collusion_with_Pakistani_security_authorities_in_the_sense_that_the_latter_consistently_monitor_the_freedom_of_movement_of_researchers,_their_contacts,_duration_of_conversations_with_informers_etc.,_and_in_consequence_the_reports,_surveys_etc._which_have_so_far_been_published_are_rather_a_result_of_what_Pakistani_security_and_administrations_have_knowingly_approved,_at_least_to_some_extent._The_mere_legalisation_and_basic_activities_of_organisations_comparable_to_those_which_have_operated_in_IaJK_(such_as_JKCCS,_APDP,_IPTK),_even_though_regularly_harassed_and_their_activists_detained,_is_hardly_imaginable_on_the_Pakistani-administered_side._Detailed_and_well-researched_reports_produced_by_) The [OHCHR reports on Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OHCHR_reports_on_Kashmir "OHCHR reports on Kashmir") released two reports on "the situation of human rights in Indian-Administered Kashmir and Pakistan-Administered Kashmir".

## India–Pakistan conflict

Further information: [Timeline of the Kashmir conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Kashmir_conflict "Timeline of the Kashmir conflict") and [Indo-Pakistani wars and conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_wars_and_conflicts "Indo-Pakistani wars and conflicts")

### Background

See also: [Jammu and Kashmir (princely state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_(princely_state) "Jammu and Kashmir (princely state)")

The Afghan [Durrani Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire "Durrani Empire") ruled Kashmir Valley from 1752[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-66) until [its 1819 conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Shopian "Battle of Shopian") by the [Sikh Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Empire "Sikh Empire") under [Ranjit Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranjit_Singh "Ranjit Singh"). The Raja of Jammu [Gulab Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulab_Singh "Gulab Singh"), who was a vassal of the Sikh Empire and an influential noble in the Sikh court, sent expeditions to various border kingdoms and ended up encircling Kashmir by 1840. Following the [First Anglo-Sikh War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Anglo-Sikh_War "First Anglo-Sikh War") (1845–1846), Kashmir was ceded under the [Treaty of Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lahore "Treaty of Lahore") to the [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company "East India Company"), which transferred it to Gulab Singh through the [Treaty of Amritsar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Amritsar,_1846 "Treaty of Amritsar, 1846"), in return for the payment of indemnity owed by the Sikh empire. Gulab Singh took the title of the Maharaja of Jammu and Kashmir.

From 1846 till the 1947 partition of India, Kashmir was ruled by maharajas of Gulab Singh's [Dogra dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogra_dynasty "Dogra dynasty"), as a [princely state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_state "Princely state") under [British Paramountcy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Paramountcy "British Paramountcy"). The British Raj managed the defence, external affairs, and communications for the princely state and stationed a British Resident in [Srinagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srinagar "Srinagar") to oversee the internal administration. According to the 1941 census, the state's population was 77 percent Muslim, 20 percent Hindu and 3 percent others (Sikhs and Buddhists).[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBose,_Kashmir_Roots_of_Conflict200327–28-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBose,_Kashmir_Roots_of_Conflict200327–28) Despite its Muslim majority, the princely rule was overwhelmingly a Hindu-dominated state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMridu_Rai,_Hindu_Rulers,_Muslim_Subjects2004-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMridu_Rai,_Hindu_Rulers,_Muslim_Subjects2004) The Muslim majority suffered under the high taxes of the administration and had few opportunities for growth and advancement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:11-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-:11)

### Partition and invasion

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Partition_of_India_1947_en.svg/250px-Partition_of_India_1947_en.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Partition_of_India_1947_en.svg) The partition of India: green regions were all part of Pakistan by 1948, and orange ones part of India.

British rule in the Indian subcontinent ended in 1947 with the creation of new states: the [dominions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion "Dominion") of [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_Pakistan "Dominion of Pakistan") and [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_India "Dominion of India"), as the successor states to [British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj "British Raj"). The British Paramountcy over the 562 Indian princely states ended.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Menon-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-Menon)[&#8202;](#ref-back-IIA1947-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-IIA1947) States were thereafter left to choose whether to join India or Pakistan or to remain independent. Jammu and Kashmir, the largest of the princely states, had a predominantly Muslim population ruled by the Hindu [Maharaja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maharaja "Maharaja") [Hari Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hari_Singh "Hari Singh"). The Maharaja decided to stay independent because he expected that the State's Muslims would be unhappy with accession to India, and the Hindus and Sikhs would become vulnerable if he joined Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kak-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-Kak)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Scott-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-Scott) On 11 August, the Maharaja dismissed his prime minister [Ram Chandra Kak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram_Chandra_Kak "Ram Chandra Kak"), who had advocated equidistance from India and Pakistan. Observers and scholars interpreted this action as a tilt towards accession to India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Scott-1)[\[70\]](#refnote-Scott)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010106-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010106) Pakistanis decided to preempt this possibility by wresting Kashmir by force if necessary.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India201031,_34–35,_105-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India201031,_34–35,_105)

Pakistan made various efforts to persuade the Maharaja of Kashmir to join Pakistan. In July 1947, [Mohammad Ali Jinnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Ali_Jinnah "Mohammad Ali Jinnah") is believed to have written to the Maharaja promising "every sort of favourable treatment", followed by the lobbying of the State's Prime Minister by leaders of Jinnah's [Muslim League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_League_(Pakistan) "Muslim League (Pakistan)") party. Faced with the Maharaja's indecision on accession, the Muslim League agents clandestinely worked in [Poonch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poonch_jagir "Poonch jagir") to encourage the [local Muslims to an armed revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947_Poonch_Rebellion "1947 Poonch Rebellion"), exploiting an internal unrest regarding economic grievances. The authorities in [Pakistani Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_Pakistan "Punjab, Pakistan") waged a 'private war' by obstructing supplies of fuel and essential commodities to the State. Later in September, Muslim League officials in the [Northwest Frontier Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Frontier_Province "Northwest Frontier Province"), including the Chief Minister [Abdul Qayyum Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Qayyum_Khan "Abdul Qayyum Khan"), assisted and possibly organized a large-scale invasion of Kashmir by [Pathan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pathan "Pathan") tribesmen.[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-76):61[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECopland,_State,_Community_and_Neighbourhood_in_Princely_India2005143-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECopland,_State,_Community_and_Neighbourhood_in_Princely_India2005143) Several sources indicate that the plans were finalised on 12 September by the Prime Minister [Liaquat Ali Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liaquat_Ali_Khan "Liaquat Ali Khan"), based on proposals prepared by Colonel [Akbar Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar_Khan_(Pakistani_general) "Akbar Khan (Pakistani general)") and Sardar [Shaukat Hayat Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaukat_Hayat_Khan "Shaukat Hayat Khan"). One plan called for organising an armed insurgency in the western districts of the state and the other for organising a [Pushtoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pushtoon "Pushtoon") tribal invasion. Both were set in motion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010105–106-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010105–106)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENawaz,_The_First_Kashmir_War_Revisited2008120–121-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENawaz,_The_First_Kashmir_War_Revisited2008120–121)

The Jammu division of the state got caught up in the Partition violence. Large numbers of Hindus and Sikhs from [Rawalpindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rawalpindi "Rawalpindi") and [Sialkot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sialkot "Sialkot") started arriving in March 1947 following [massacres in Rawalpindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947_Rawalpindi_massacres "1947 Rawalpindi massacres"), bringing "harrowing stories of Muslim atrocities." According to Ilyas Chattha, this provoked [counter-violence on Jammu Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947_Jammu_massacres "1947 Jammu massacres"), which had "many parallels with that in Sialkot."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEChattha,_Partition_and_its_Aftermath2009179–180-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEChattha,_Partition_and_its_Aftermath2009179–180) The violence in the eastern districts of Jammu that started in September, developed into a widespread [massacre of Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947_Jammu_massacres "1947 Jammu massacres") around October, organised by the Hindu Dogra troops of the State and perpetrated by the local Hindus, including members of the [Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashtriya_Swayamsevak_Sangh "Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh"), and the Hindus and Sikhs displaced from the neighbouring areas of West Pakistan. The Maharaja himself was implicated in some instances. A large number of Muslims were killed. Others fled to West Pakistan, some of whom made their way to the western districts of Poonch and Mirpur, which were undergoing rebellion. Many of these Muslims believed that the Maharaja ordered the killings in Jammu which instigated the Muslims in West Pakistan to join the uprising in Poonch and help in the formation of the Azad Kashmir government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201348–57-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201348–57)

The rebel forces in the western districts of Jammu were organised under the leadership of [Sardar Ibrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardar_Ibrahim "Sardar Ibrahim"), a [Muslim Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Muslim_Conference "All Jammu and Kashmir Muslim Conference") leader. They took control of most of the western parts of the State by 22 October. On 24 October, they formed a provisional [Azad Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Kashmir "Azad Kashmir") (free Kashmir) government based in [Palandri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palandri "Palandri").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201345-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201345)

### Accession

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Admiral_Lord_Louis_Mountbatten%2C_1943._TR1230.jpg/250px-Admiral_Lord_Louis_Mountbatten%2C_1943._TR1230.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Admiral_Lord_Louis_Mountbatten,_1943._TR1230.jpg) The [Instrument of Accession of Kashmir to India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Accession_(Jammu_and_Kashmir) "Instrument of Accession (Jammu and Kashmir)") was accepted by the Governor General of India, [Lord Mountbatten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Mountbatten "Lord Mountbatten").

Justice [Mehr Chand Mahajan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehr_Chand_Mahajan "Mehr Chand Mahajan"), the Maharaja's nominee for his next prime minister, visited [Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru "Jawaharlal Nehru") and [Patel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vallabhbhai_Patel "Vallabhbhai Patel") in Delhi on 19 September 1947, requesting essential supplies which had been blockaded by Pakistan since the beginning of September. He communicated the Maharaja's willingness to accede to India. Nehru, however, demanded that the jailed political leader, [Sheikh Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Abdullah "Sheikh Abdullah"), be released from prison and involved in the state government. Only then would he allow the state to accede.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010105-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010105)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEJha,_The_Origins_of_a_Dispute200347-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEJha,_The_Origins_of_a_Dispute200347) The Maharaja released Sheikh Abdullah on 29 September.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010106-1)[\[71\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010106)

The Maharaja's troops could not withstand the tribal militia attack in September and October 1947; they were heavily outnumbered and outgunned by the tribal militias, and were also facing internal rebellions from Muslim troops. The Maharaja made an urgent plea to Delhi for military assistance. Upon the Governor General [Lord Mountbatten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Mountbatten "Lord Mountbatten")'s insistence, India required the Maharaja to accede before it could send troops. Accordingly, the Maharaja signed an [instrument of accession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Accession_(Jammu_and_Kashmir) "Instrument of Accession (Jammu and Kashmir)") on 26 October 1947, which was accepted by the Governor General the next day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010108-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010108)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEJha,_The_Origins_of_a_Dispute200369-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEJha,_The_Origins_of_a_Dispute200369)[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-87) While the Government of India accepted the accession, it added the proviso that it would be submitted to a "reference to the people" after the state is cleared of the invaders, since "only the people, not the Maharaja, could decide where Kashmiris wanted to live."; it was a provisional accession.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEVarshney,_Three_Compromised_Nationalisms1992194-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEVarshney,_Three_Compromised_Nationalisms1992194)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Khan2010-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-Khan2010)[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[note 4\]](#refnote-91) The largest political party, [National Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_National_Conference "Jammu and Kashmir National Conference"), headed by Sheikh Abdullah, endorsed the accession. In the words of the National Conference leader [Syed Mir Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syed_Mir_Qasim "Syed Mir Qasim"), India had the "legal" as well as "moral" justification to send in the army through the Maharaja's accession and the people's support of it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPanigrahi,_Jammu_and_Kashmir,_the_Cold_War_and_the_West200954-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPanigrahi,_Jammu_and_Kashmir,_the_Cold_War_and_the_West200954)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Panigrahi54-0)[\[note 5\]](#refnote-Panigrahi54)

The Indian troops, which were airlifted in the early hours of 27 October, secured the Srinagar airport. The city of Srinagar was being patrolled by the National Conference volunteers with Hindus and Sikhs moving about freely among Muslims, an "incredible sight" to visiting journalists. The National Conference also worked with the Indian Army to secure the city.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGuha,_India_after_Gandhi2008xx-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGuha,_India_after_Gandhi2008xx)

In the north of the state lay the [Gilgit Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilgit_Agency "Gilgit Agency"), which had been leased by British India but returned to the Maharaja shortly before Independence. Gilgit's population did not favour the State's accession to India. Sensing their discontent, Major William Brown, the Maharaja's commander of the [Gilgit Scouts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilgit_Scouts "Gilgit Scouts"), [mutinied on 1 November 1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947_Gilgit_rebellion "1947 Gilgit rebellion"), overthrowing the Governor Ghansara Singh. The bloodless _coup d'état_ was planned by Brown to the last detail under the code name " _Datta Khel_". Local leaders in Gilgit formed a provisional government ( _Aburi Hakoomat_), naming Raja Shah Rais Khan as the president and [Mirza Hassan Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirza_Hassan_Khan "Mirza Hassan Khan") as the commander-in-chief, but Major Brown had already telegraphed [Khan Abdul Qayyum Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khan_Abdul_Qayyum_Khan "Khan Abdul Qayyum Khan") asking Pakistan to take over. According to historian Yaqoob Khan Bangash, the provisional government lacked sway over the population which had intense pro-Pakistan sentiments.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-95) Pakistan's Political Agent, Khan Mohammad Alam Khan, arrived on 16 November and took over the administration of Gilgit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200363–64-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200363–64)[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-97) According to various scholars, the people of Gilgit as well as those of Chilas, Koh Ghizr, Ishkoman, Yasin, Punial, [Hunza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunza_(princely_state) "Hunza (princely state)") and [Nagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagar_(princely_state) "Nagar (princely state)") joined Pakistan by choice.[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-98)[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-99)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Zutshi2004-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-Zutshi2004)[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-101)

### Indo-Pakistani War of 1947

Main article: [Indo-Pakistani War of 1947–1948](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1947%E2%80%931948 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1947–1948")

Rebel forces from the western districts of the State and the Pakistani Pathan tribesmen[&#8202;](#ref-back-Snedden46-0)[\[note 6\]](#refnote-Snedden46)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Snedden68-0)[\[note 7\]](#refnote-Snedden68) made rapid advances into the [Baramulla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baramulla_district "Baramulla district") sector. In the Kashmir Valley, [National Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_&_Kashmir_National_Conference "Jammu & Kashmir National Conference") volunteers worked with the [Indian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Army "Indian Army") to drive out the 'raiders'.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sayyid_Mir_Qasim-0)[\[note 8\]](#refnote-Sayyid_Mir_Qasim) The resulting [First Kashmir War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Kashmir_War "First Kashmir War") lasted until the end of 1948.

The Pakistan army made available arms, ammunition and supplies to the rebel forces who were dubbed the "Azad Army". Pakistani army officers "conveniently" on leave and the former officers of the [Indian National Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Army "Indian National Army") were recruited to command the forces. In May 1948, the Pakistani army officially entered the conflict, in theory to defend the Pakistan borders, but it made plans to push towards Jammu and cut the lines of communications of the Indian forces in the [Mendhar Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mendhar_Tehsil "Mendhar Tehsil").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200365–67-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200365–67) [C. Christine Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Christine_Fair "C. Christine Fair") notes that this was the beginning of Pakistan using irregular forces and "asymmetric warfare" to ensure [plausible deniability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plausible_deniability "Plausible deniability"), which has continued ever since.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEFair,_Militant_Challenge_in_Pakistan2011107–108-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEFair,_Militant_Challenge_in_Pakistan2011107–108)

On 1 November 1947, Mountbatten flew to Lahore for a conference with Jinnah, proposing that, in all the princely States where the ruler did not accede to a Dominion corresponding to the majority population (which would have included [Junagadh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junagadh "Junagadh"), [Hyderabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyderabad "Hyderabad") as well as Kashmir), the accession should be decided by an "impartial reference to the will of the people". Jinnah rejected the offer. According to Indian scholar [A. G. Noorani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._G._Noorani "A. G. Noorani"), Jinnah ended up squandering his leverage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-107)

Azad Kashmir forces and [Pashtun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun "Pashtun") tribesmen captured [Rajouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajouri "Rajouri") on 7 November 1947, and over the following months subjected the city's Hindu population to lootings and killing, with few Hindu civilians managing to avoid to ordeal by either escaping the city or taking refuge among sympathetic Muslim families in nearby villages. Fear of abduction and sexual abuse led to many women dying in mass suicides and killings carried out "by their own menfolk". The situation continued until April 1948, when the Indian Armed Forces retook the city.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBhatia2020[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidjxzhDwAAQBAJpgPA82_80–82]-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBhatia2020[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidjxzhDwAAQBAJpgPA82_80–82])

On 25 November, the Pakistani tribesmen and soldiers attacked and took over [Mirpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirpur,_Pakistan "Mirpur, Pakistan"), and began the [Mirpur Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirpur_Massacre "Mirpur Massacre") of Hindus and Sikhs in the area. An estimated 20,000+ Hindus and Sikhs were killed overall. Rapes and other crimes were also committed during the aftermath.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir201297-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir201297)

According to Jinnah, India acquired the accession through "fraud and violence".[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200361-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200361) A plebiscite was unnecessary and states should accede according to their majority population. He was willing to urge Junagadh to accede to India in return for Kashmir. For a plebiscite, Jinnah demanded simultaneous troop withdrawal for he felt that 'the average Muslim would never have the courage to vote for Pakistan' in the presence of Indian troops and with Sheikh Abdullah in power. When Mountbatten countered that the plebiscite could be conducted by the United Nations, Jinnah, hoping that the invasion would succeed and Pakistan might lose a plebiscite, again rejected the proposal, stating that the Governors General should conduct it instead. Mountbatten noted that it was untenable given his constitutional position and India did not accept Jinnah's demand of removing Sheikh Abdullah.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010111-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010111)[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[note 9\]](#refnote-112)
