Prime Ministers Nehru and [Liaquat Ali Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liaquat_Ali_Khan "Liaquat Ali Khan") met again in December, when Nehru informed Khan of India's intention to refer the dispute to the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations "United Nations") under article 35 of the UN Charter, which allows the member states to bring to the Security Council attention situations 'likely to endanger the maintenance of international peace'.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200367–68-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200367–68)

Nehru and other Indian leaders were afraid since 1947 that the "temporary" accession to India might act as an irritant to the bulk of the Muslims of Kashmir. V.P. Menon, Secretary in Patel's Ministry of States, admitted in an interview in 1964 that India had been absolutely dishonest on the issue of plebiscite.[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-114) A.G. Noorani blames many Indian and Pakistani leaders for the misery of Kashmiri people but says that Nehru was the main culprit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-115)

### UN mediation

Main article: [UN mediation of the Kashmir dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_mediation_of_the_Kashmir_dispute "UN mediation of the Kashmir dispute")

India sought resolution of the issue at the [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council "UN Security Council"), despite Sheikh Abdullah's opposition to it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sayyid_Mir_Qasim-1)[\[note 8\]](#refnote-Sayyid_Mir_Qasim) Following the set-up of the [United Nations Commission for India and Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Commission_for_India_and_Pakistan "United Nations Commission for India and Pakistan") (UNCIP), the UN Security Council passed [Resolution 47](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_47 "United Nations Security Council Resolution 47") on 21 April 1948. The measure called for an immediate cease-fire and called on the Government of Pakistan 'to secure the withdrawal from the state of Jammu and Kashmir of tribesmen and Pakistani nationals not normally resident therein who have entered the state for the purpose of fighting.' It also asked Government of India to reduce its forces to minimum strength, after which the circumstances for holding a [plebiscite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plebiscite "Plebiscite") should be put into effect 'on the question of Accession of the state to India or Pakistan.' However, it was not until 1 January 1949 that the ceasefire could be put into effect, signed by General [Douglas Gracey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Gracey "Douglas Gracey") on behalf of Pakistan and General [Roy Bucher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_Bucher "Roy Bucher") on behalf of India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200368–69-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200368–69) However, both India and Pakistan failed to arrive at a truce agreement due to differences over interpretation of the procedure for and the extent of demilitarisation. One sticking point was whether the Azad Kashmiri army was to be disbanded during the truce stage or at the plebiscite stage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-117)

The UNCIP made three visits to the subcontinent between 1948 and 1949, trying to find a solution agreeable to both India and Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200370-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200370) It reported to the Security Council in August 1948 that "the presence of troops of Pakistan" inside Kashmir represented a "material change" in the situation. A two-part process was proposed for the withdrawal of forces. In the first part, Pakistan was to withdraw its forces as well as other Pakistani nationals from the state. In the second part, "when the Commission shall have notified the Government of India" that Pakistani withdrawal has been completed, India was to withdraw the bulk of its forces. After both the withdrawals were completed, a plebiscite would be held.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEVarshney,_Three_Compromised_Nationalisms1992211-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEVarshney,_Three_Compromised_Nationalisms1992211)[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[note 10\]](#refnote-120) The resolution was accepted by India but effectively rejected by Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Korbel502-0)[\[note 11\]](#refnote-Korbel502)

The Indian government considered itself to be under legal possession of Jammu and Kashmir by virtue of the accession of the state. The assistance given by Pakistan to the rebel forces and the Pashtun tribes was held to be a hostile act and the further involvement of the Pakistan army was taken to be an invasion of Indian territory. From the Indian perspective, the plebiscite was meant to confirm the accession, which was in all respects already complete, and Pakistan could not aspire to an equal footing with India in the contest.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200370–71-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200370–71)

The Pakistani government held that the state of Jammu and Kashmir had executed a [standstill agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standstill_agreement_(India) "Standstill agreement (India)") with Pakistan which precluded it from entering into agreements with other countries. It also held that the Maharaja had no authority left to execute accession because his people had revolted and he had to flee the capital. Its position was that the Azad Kashmir movement, as well as the tribal incursions, were indigenous and spontaneous, and Pakistan's assistance to them was not open to criticism.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200371–72-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200371–72)

In short, India required an asymmetric treatment of the two countries in the withdrawal arrangements, regarding Pakistan as an 'aggressor', whereas Pakistan insisted on parity. The UN mediators tended towards parity, which was not to India's satisfaction.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200382–85-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200382–85) In the end, no withdrawal was ever carried out, India insisting that Pakistan had to withdraw first, and Pakistan contending that there was no guarantee that India would withdraw afterwards.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEVarshney,_Three_Compromised_Nationalisms1992212-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEVarshney,_Three_Compromised_Nationalisms1992212) No agreement could be reached between the two countries on the process of demilitarisation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Korbel506-0)[\[note 12\]](#refnote-Korbel506)

Cold War historian [Robert J. McMahon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_J._McMahon "Robert J. McMahon") states that American officials increasingly blamed India for rejecting various UNCIP truce proposals under various dubious legal technicalities just to avoid a plebiscite. According to McMahon, they were right, since a Muslim majority made a vote to join Pakistan the "most likely outcome" and postponing the plebiscite would serve India's interests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-McMahon2010-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-McMahon2010)

Scholars have commented that the failure of the Security Council efforts of mediation owed to the fact that the Council regarded the issue as a purely political dispute without investigating its legal underpinnings.[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[note 13\]](#refnote-128) Declassified British papers indicate that Britain and the US had let their Cold War calculations influence their policy in the UN, disregarding the merits of the case.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ankit2013-0)[\[note 14\]](#refnote-Ankit2013)

### Dixon Plan

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Dixon_01.jpg/250px-Dixon_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dixon_01.jpg) Sir [Owen Dixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owen_Dixon "Owen Dixon"), UN mediator

The UNCIP appointed its successor, Sir [Owen Dixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owen_Dixon "Owen Dixon"), to implement demilitarisation prior to a statewide plebiscite on the basis of General McNaughton's scheme, and to recommend solutions to the two governments.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012156–-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012156–)[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-131)[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-132) Dixon's efforts for a statewide plebiscite came to naught due to India's constant rejection of the various alternative demilitarisation proposals, for which Dixon rebuked India harshly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012160–-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012160–)

Dixon then offered an alternative proposal, widely known as the Dixon plan. Dixon did not view the state of Jammu and Kashmir as one homogeneous unit and therefore proposed that a plebiscite be limited to the Valley. Dixon agreed that people in Jammu and Ladakh were clearly in favour of India; equally clearly, those in Azad Kashmir and the Northern Areas wanted to be part of Pakistan. This left the Kashmir Valley and 'perhaps some adjacent country' around Muzaffarabad in uncertain political terrain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Snedden_plebiscite-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-Snedden_plebiscite) Pakistan did not accept this plan because it believed that India's commitment to a plebiscite for the whole state should not be abandoned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012161–-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012161–)[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-136)[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-137)

Dixon also had concerns that the Kashmiris, not being high-spirited people, may vote under fear or improper influences.[&#8202;](#ref-back-138-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-138) Following Pakistan's objections, he proposed that Sheikh Abdullah administration should be held in "commission" (in abeyance) while the plebiscite was held. This was not acceptable to India which rejected the Dixon plan. Another grounds for India's rejection of the limited plebiscite was that it wanted Indian troops to remain in Kashmir for "security purposes", but would not allow Pakistani troops the same. However, Dixon's plan had encapsulated a withdrawal by both sides. Dixon had believed a neutral administration would be essential for a fair plebiscite.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012161–162-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012161–162)

Dixon came to the conclusion that India would never agree to conditions and a demilitarization which would ensure a free and fair plebiscite.[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-140)[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-141) Dixon's failure also compounded American ambassador Loy Henderson's misgivings about Indian sincerity and he advised the US to maintain a distance from the Kashmir dispute, which the US subsequently did, and leave the matter for Commonwealth nations to intervene in.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchaffer,_The_Limits_of_Influence200930–-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchaffer,_The_Limits_of_Influence200930–)

### 1950 military standoff

The convening of the Constituent Assembly in Indian Kashmir in July 1950 proved contentious. Pakistan protested to the Security Council which informed India that this development conflicted with the parties' commitments. The National Conference rejected this resolution and Nehru supported this by telling Dr Graham that he would receive no help in implementing the Resolution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBrecher1953119-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBrecher1953119) A month later Nehru adopted a more conciliatory attitude, telling a press conference that the Assembly's actions would not affect India's plebiscite commitment. The delay caused frustration in Pakistan and Zafrullah Khan went on to say that Pakistan was not keeping a warlike mentality but did not know what Indian intransigence would lead Pakistan and its people to. India accused Pakistan of ceasefire violations and Nehru complained of 'warmongering propaganda' in Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBrecher1953120-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBrecher1953120) On 15 July 1951 the Pakistani Prime Minister complained that the bulk of the Indian Army was concentrated on the Indo-Pakistan border.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBrecher1953121-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBrecher1953121)

The prime ministers of the two countries exchanged telegrams accusing each other of bad intentions. Liaquat Ali Khan rejected Nehru's charge of warmongering propaganda.[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[note 15\]](#refnote-146) Khan called it a distortion of the Pakistani press' discontent with India over its persistence in not holding a plebiscite and a misrepresentation of the desire to liberate Kashmir as an anti-Indian war. Khan also accused India of raising its defence budget in the past two years, a charge which Nehru rejected while expressing surprise at Khan's dismissal of the 'virulent' anti-Indian propaganda. Khan and Nehru also disagreed on the details of the no-war declarations. Khan then submitted a peace plan calling for a withdrawal of troops, settlement in Kashmir by plebiscite, renouncing the use of force, end to war propaganda and the signing of a no-war pact.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBrecher1953122-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBrecher1953122) Nehru did not accept the second and third components of this peace plan. The peace plan failed. While an opposition leader in Pakistan did call for war, leaders in both India and Pakistan did urge calm to avert disaster.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBrecher1953123-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBrecher1953123)

The Commonwealth had taken up the Kashmir issue in January 1951. Australian prime minister Robert Menzies suggested that a Commonwealth force be stationed in Kashmir; that a joint Indo-Pakistani force be stationed in Kashmir and the plebiscite administrator be entitled to raise local troops while the plebiscite would be held. Pakistan accepted these proposals but India rejected them because it did not want Pakistan, who was in India's eyes the 'aggressor', to have an equal footing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-149) The UN Security Council called on India and Pakistan to honour the resolutions of plebiscite both had accepted in 1948 and 1949. The United States and Britain proposed that if the two could not reach an agreement then arbitration would be considered. Pakistan agreed but Nehru said he would not allow a third person to decide the fate of four million people. Korbel criticised India's stance towards a ″valid″ and ″recommended technique of international co-operation.″[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200383–86-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200383–86)[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-151)

However, the peace was short-lived. Later by 1953, Sheikh Abdullah, who was by then in favour of resolving Kashmir by a plebiscite, an idea which was "anathema" to the Indian government according to historian Zutshi,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEZutshi,_Languages_of_Belonging2004321-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEZutshi,_Languages_of_Belonging2004321) fell out with the Indian government. He was dismissed and imprisoned in August 1953. His former deputy, [Bakshi Ghulam Mohammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakshi_Ghulam_Mohammad "Bakshi Ghulam Mohammad") was subsequently appointed as the [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "List of Chief Ministers of Jammu and Kashmir"), and Indian security forces were deployed in the Valley to control the streets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010225-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010225)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir20166–7-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir20166–7)

### Nehru's plebiscite offer

In May 1953, the US Secretary of State [John Foster Dulles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Foster_Dulles "John Foster Dulles") recommended India and Pakistan seek a bilateral solution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERizvi,_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Kashmir_Problem199257-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERizvi,_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Kashmir_Problem199257)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchaffer,_The_Limits_of_Influence200938-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchaffer,_The_Limits_of_Influence200938) Around this time, Sheikh Abdullah fell out with the Indian government and lost the support of his colleageues in his cabinet. He was dismissed and imprisoned in August 1953. His former deputy, [Bakshi Ghulam Mohammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakshi_Ghulam_Mohammad "Bakshi Ghulam Mohammad") was appointed as the prime minister, and Indian security forces were deployed in the Valley to control the streets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010225-1)[\[138\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010225)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir20166–7-1)[\[139\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir20166–7)

With India's "abridged authority" in Kashmir, Nehru decided that a settlement must be found. India could not hold Kashmir "at the point of a bayonet". Starting in July 1953, he made a renewed push on the plebiscite option in discussions with Pakistan. In bilateral talks held in Delhi in August 1953, he proposed that a plebiscite administrator be appointed within six months. Other than demanding that the plebiscite administrator not be from one of the major powers, he placed no other conditions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010225-2)[\[138\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010225)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir20166–7-2)[\[139\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir20166–7) Historian Gowher Rizvi notes a "dramatic reversal" of India's earlier position. "Nehru was now willing to offer virtually everything that Pakistan had been seeking since 1947".[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERizvi,_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Kashmir_Problem199258-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERizvi,_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Kashmir_Problem199258) Nehru suggested that the plebiscite could be held in all regions of the state and the state could be partitioned on the basis of the results. He was open to a "different approach" to the scaling back of troops in the State so as to allow a free vote.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010225-3)[\[138\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010225)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir20166–7-3)[\[139\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir20166–7)

Pakistani prime minister Bogra was able to return home triumphantly. However, in the face of questions and criticisms from colleagues, his commitment began to waver. The main objection from the Pakistani leaders was to Nehru's demand for replacing the plebiscite administrator ( [Admiral Nimitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester_W._Nimitz "Chester W. Nimitz"), appointed by the UN Security Council) with somebody from a smaller neutral power that had no strategic interests in the region. Pakistanis suspected sinister motives and time was whittled away.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERizvi,_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Kashmir_Problem199258–59-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERizvi,_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Kashmir_Problem199258–59)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchaffer,_The_Limits_of_Influence200943-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchaffer,_The_Limits_of_Influence200943)

### Cold War

The USA in February 1954 announced that it wanted to provide military aid to Pakistan. The US signed a military pact with Pakistan in May by which Pakistan would receive military equipment and training. The US president tried to alleviate India's concerns by offering similar weaponry to India. This was an unsuccessful attempt.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchaffer,_The_Limits_of_Influence200946–-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchaffer,_The_Limits_of_Influence200946–) Nehru's misgivings about the US-Pakistan pact made him hostile to a plebiscite.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-auto) Consequently, when the pact was concluded in May 1954, Nehru withdrew the plebiscite offer and declared that the _status quo_ was the only remaining option.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir201612–13-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir201612–13)

Nehru's withdrawal from the plebiscite option came as a major blow to all concerned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir201612-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir201612) Scholars have suggested that India was never seriously intent on holding a plebiscite.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir20166-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEShankar,_Nehru's_Legacy_in_Kashmir20166)[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-168)[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-169)

Indian writer [Nirad C. Chaudhuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirad_C._Chaudhuri "Nirad C. Chaudhuri") has observed that Pakistan's acceptance of Western support ensured its survival.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200385,_257-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200385,_257) He believed that India intended to invade Pakistan twice or thrice during the period 1947–1954. For scholar Wayne Wilcox, Pakistan was able to find external support to counter "Hindu superiority", returning to the group security position of the early 20th century.[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-171)

### Sino-Indian War

Main article: [Sino-Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_War "Sino-Indian War")

In 1962, troops from the [People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Republic_of_China "People's Republic of China") and India clashed in territory claimed by both. China won a swift victory in the war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-172) [Aksai Chin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aksai_Chin "Aksai Chin"), part of which was under Chinese jurisdiction before the war,[&#8202;](#ref-back-middlepath-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-middlepath)[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-174)[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-175)[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-176) remained under Chinese control since then. Another smaller area, the [Trans-Karakoram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Karakoram "Trans-Karakoram"), was demarcated as the [Line of Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Control "Line of Control") (LOC) between China and Pakistan, although some of the territory on the Chinese side is claimed by India to be part of Kashmir. The line that separates India from China in this region is known as the " [Line of Actual Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Actual_Control "Line of Actual Control")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-177-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-177)

### Operation Gibraltar and 1965 Indo-Pakistani war

Main articles: [Operation Gibraltar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Gibraltar "Operation Gibraltar"), [Indo-Pakistani War of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1965 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1965"), and [Tashkent Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tashkent_Declaration "Tashkent Declaration")

Following its failure to seize Kashmir in 1947, Pakistan supported numerous 'covert cells' in Kashmir using operatives based in its New Delhi embassy. After its military pact with the United States in the 1950s, it intensively studied guerrilla warfare through engagement with the US military. In 1965, it decided that the conditions were ripe for a successful guerilla war in Kashmir. Code named ' [Operation Gibraltar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Gibraltar "Operation Gibraltar")', companies were dispatched into Indian-administered Kashmir, the majority of whose members were _razakars_ (volunteers) and _mujahideen_ recruited from Pakistani-administered Kashmir and trained by the Army. These irregular forces were supported by officers and men from the paramilitary [Northern Light Infantry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Light_Infantry "Northern Light Infantry") and [Azad Kashmir Rifles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Kashmir_Regiment "Azad Kashmir Regiment") as well as commandos from the [Special Services Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Services_Group "Special Services Group"). About 30,000 infiltrators are estimated to have been dispatched in August 1965 as part of the 'Operation Gibraltar'.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEFair,_Militant_Challenge_in_Pakistan2011109–111-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEFair,_Militant_Challenge_in_Pakistan2011109–111)

The plan was for the infiltrators to mingle with the local populace and incite them to rebellion. Meanwhile, guerilla warfare would commence, destroying bridges, tunnels and highways, as well as Indian Army installations and airfields, creating conditions for an 'armed insurrection' in Kashmir.[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-179) If the attempt failed, Pakistan hoped to have raised international attention to the Kashmir issue.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPaul,_Asymmetric_Conflicts1994107-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPaul,_Asymmetric_Conflicts1994107) Using the newly acquired sophisticated weapons through the American arms aid, Pakistan believed that it could achieve tactical victories in a quick limited war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPaul,_Asymmetric_Conflicts1994115–116-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPaul,_Asymmetric_Conflicts1994115–116)

However, the 'Operation Gibraltar' ended in failure as the Kashmiris did not revolt. Instead, they turned in infiltrators to the Indian authorities in substantial numbers, and the Indian Army ended up fighting the Pakistani Army regulars. Pakistan claimed that the captured men were Kashmiri 'freedom fighters', a claim contradicted by the international media.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mankekar1967-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-Mankekar1967)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Varshney_216-0)[\[note 16\]](#refnote-Varshney_216)
On 1 September, Pakistan launched an attack across the Cease Fire Line, targeting [Akhnoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhnoor "Akhnoor") in an effort to cut Indian communications into Kashmir. In response, India broadened the war by launching an attack on [Pakistani Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_Pakistan "Punjab, Pakistan") across the international border. The war lasted until 23 September, ending in a stalemate. Following the [Tashkent Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tashkent_Agreement "Tashkent Agreement"), both the sides withdrew to their pre-conflict positions, and agreed not to interfere in each other's internal affairs.

### 1971 Indo-Pakistani war and Simla Agreement

Main articles: [Indo-Pakistani War of 1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1971") and [Simla Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_Agreement "Simla Agreement")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Un-kashmir-jammu.png/250px-Un-kashmir-jammu.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Un-kashmir-jammu.png) The [Line of Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Control "Line of Control") between India and Pakistan agreed in the Simla Agreement (UN Map)

The [Indo-Pakistani War of 1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1971") led to a loss for Pakistan and a military surrender in [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan "East Pakistan"). [Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh "Bangladesh") was created as a separate state with India's support and India emerged as a clear regional power in South Asia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGanguly,_Crisis_in_Kashmir199960-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGanguly,_Crisis_in_Kashmir199960)

A bilateral summit was held at [Simla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla "Simla") as a follow-up to the war, where India pushed for peace in South Asia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEDixit,_India-Pakistan_in_War_and_Peace2003228–229-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEDixit,_India-Pakistan_in_War_and_Peace2003228–229)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGanguly,_Crisis_in_Kashmir199960–63-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGanguly,_Crisis_in_Kashmir199960–63) At stake were 5,139 square miles (13,310km2) of Pakistan's territory captured by India during the conflict, and over 90,000 prisoners of war held in Bangladesh. India was ready to return them in exchange for a "durable solution" to the Kashmir issue. Diplomat [J. N. Dixit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._N._Dixit "J. N. Dixit") states that the negotiations at Simla were painful and tortuous, and almost broke down. The deadlock was broken in a personal meeting between the Prime Ministers [Zulfikar Ali Bhutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulfikar_Ali_Bhutto "Zulfikar Ali Bhutto") and [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi "Indira Gandhi"), where Bhutto acknowledged that the Kashmir issue should be finally resolved and removed as a hurdle in India-Pakistan relations; that the cease-fire line, to be renamed the [Line of Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Control "Line of Control"), could be gradually converted into a _de jure_ border between India and Pakistan; and that he would take steps to integrate the Pakistani-controlled portions of Jammu and Kashmir into the federal territories of Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEDixit,_India-Pakistan_in_War_and_Peace2003228–229-1)[\[169\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEDixit,_India-Pakistan_in_War_and_Peace2003228–229) However, he requested that the formal declaration of the Agreement should not include a final settlement of the Kashmir dispute as it would endanger his fledgling civilian government and bring in military and other hardline elements into power in Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-187)

Accordingly, the [Simla Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_Agreement "Simla Agreement") was formulated and signed by the two countries, whereby the countries resolved to settle their differences by peaceful means through bilateral negotiations and to maintain the sanctity of the Line of Control. Multilateral negotiations were not ruled out, but they were conditional upon both sides agreeing to them.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Cohen_2002-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-Cohen_2002):49–50 To India, this meant an end to the UN or other multilateral negotiations. However Pakistan reinterpreted the wording in the light of a reference to the "UN charter" in the agreement, and maintained that it could still approach the UN. The United States, United Kingdom and most Western governments agree with India's interpretation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-189-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-189)

The Simla Agreement also stated that the two sides would meet again for establishing durable peace. Reportedly Bhutto asked for time to prepare the people of Pakistan and the National Assembly for a final settlement. Indian commentators state that he reneged on the promise. Bhutto told the National Assembly on 14 July that he forged an equal agreement from an unequal beginning and that he did not compromise on the right of self-determination for Jammu and Kashmir. The envisioned meeting never occurred.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGuha,_India_after_Gandhi2008Sec.&nbsp;20.VII-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGuha,_India_after_Gandhi2008Sec.&nbsp;20.VII)

### Kargil War (1999)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Kargil.map.gif/250px-Kargil.map.gif?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kargil.map.gif) Location of starting the war

Main article: [Kargil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_War "Kargil War")

In mid-1999, alleged insurgents and Pakistani soldiers from [Pakistani Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Areas,_Pakistan "Northern Areas, Pakistan") infiltrated Jammu and Kashmir. During the winter season, Indian forces regularly move down to lower altitudes, as severe climatic conditions makes it almost impossible for them to guard the high peaks near the Line of Control. This practice is followed by both India and Pakistan Army. The terrain makes it difficult for both sides to maintain a strict border control over Line of Control. The insurgents took advantage of this and occupied vacant mountain peaks in the [Kargil range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_district "Kargil district") overlooking the highway in Indian Kashmir that connects [Srinagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srinagar "Srinagar") and [Leh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leh "Leh"). By blocking the highway, they could cut off the only link between the Kashmir Valley and [Ladakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladakh "Ladakh"). This resulted in a large-scale conflict between the Indian and Pakistani armies. The final stage involved major battles by Indian and Pakistani forces, with India recapturing most of the territories[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-191)[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-192) held by Pakistani forces.

Fears of the [Kargil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_War "Kargil War") turning into a [nuclear war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_warfare "Nuclear warfare") provoked the then-United States President [Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Clinton "Bill Clinton") to pressure Pakistan to retreat. The Pakistan Army withdrew their remaining troops from the area, ending the conflict. India regained control of the Kargil peaks, which they now patrol and monitor all year long.

## Internal conflict

See also: [Timeline of the Kashmir conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Kashmir_conflict "Timeline of the Kashmir conflict")

### Political movements during the Dogra rule (1846–1947)

Main article: [Political movements in Jammu and Kashmir (princely state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_movements_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir_(princely_state) "Political movements in Jammu and Kashmir (princely state)")

See also: [Timeline of the Kashmir conflict §1846–1945: Princely state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Kashmir_conflict#1846%E2%80%931945:_Princely_state "Timeline of the Kashmir conflict")

In 1932 [Sheikh Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Abdullah "Sheikh Abdullah"), a Kashmiri, and [Chaudhry Ghulam Abbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaudhry_Ghulam_Abbas "Chaudhry Ghulam Abbas"), a Jammuite, led the founding of the _[All-Jammu and Kashmir Muslim Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Muslim_Conference "All Jammu and Kashmir Muslim Conference")_ in order to agitate for the rights of Muslims in the state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBehera,_Demystifying_Kashmir200716-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBehera,_Demystifying_Kashmir200716) In 1938, they renamed the party [National Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_&_Kashmir_National_Conference "Jammu & Kashmir National Conference") in order to make it representative of all Kashmiris independent of religion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGuha,_Opening_a_Window_in_Kashmir200480-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGuha,_Opening_a_Window_in_Kashmir200480)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPuri,_Across_the_Line_of_Control201316-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPuri,_Across_the_Line_of_Control201316) The move brought Abdullah closer to [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru "Jawaharlal Nehru"), the rising leader of the Congress party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200321-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200321) The National Conference eventually became a leading member of the _[All-India States Peoples' Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_States_Peoples'_Conference "All India States Peoples' Conference")_, a Congress-sponsored confederation of the political movements in the princely states.

Three years later, rifts developed within the Conference owing to political, regional and ideological differences. A faction of the party's leadership grew disenchanted with Abdullah's leanings towards Nehru and the Congress, and his secularisation of Kashmiri politics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201323-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201323)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Low1991-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-Low1991)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBehera,_Demystifying_Kashmir200719-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBehera,_Demystifying_Kashmir200719)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Snedden2015-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-Snedden2015) Consequently, Abbas broke away from the National Conference and revived the old [Muslim Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Muslim_Conference "All Jammu and Kashmir Muslim Conference") in 1941, in collaboration with [Mirwaiz Yusuf Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirwaiz_Yusuf_Shah "Mirwaiz Yusuf Shah"). These developments indicated fissures between the ethnic [Kashmiris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiris "Kashmiris") and Jammuites, as well as between the Hindus and Muslims of Jammu.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPuri,_Across_the_Line_of_Control201316–17-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPuri,_Across_the_Line_of_Control201316–17) Muslims in the Jammu region were Punjabi-speaking and felt closer affinity to [Punjabi Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_Muslims "Punjabi Muslims") than with the Valley Kashmiris.[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-202) In due course, the Muslim Conference started aligning itself ideologically with the [All-India Muslim League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-India_Muslim_League "All-India Muslim League"), and supported its [call for an independent 'Pakistan'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_Resolution "Lahore Resolution").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201323-1)[\[181\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201323) The Muslim Conference derived popular support among the Muslims of the Jammu region, and some from the Valley.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200322–23-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200322–23)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201324-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201324) Conversely, Abdullah's National Conference enjoyed influence in the Valley.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201324-1)[\[188\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201324) [Chitralekha Zutshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chitralekha_Zutshi "Chitralekha Zutshi") states that the political loyalties of Valley Kashmiris were divided in 1947, but the Muslim Conference failed to capitalise on it due its fractiousness and the lack of a distinct political programme.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEZutshi,_Languages_of_Belonging2004299-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEZutshi,_Languages_of_Belonging2004299)

In 1946, the National Conference launched the 'Quit Kashmir' movement, asking the Maharaja to hand the power over to the people. The movement came under criticism from the Muslim Conference, who charged that Abdullah was doing it to boost his own popularity, waning because of his pro-India stance. Instead, the Muslim Conference launched a 'campaign of action' similar to Muslim League's programme in British India. Both Abdullah and Abbas were imprisoned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200324-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200324) By 22 July 1947, the Muslim Conference started calling for the state's accession to Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201325-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201325)

The [Dogra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogras "Dogras") Hindus of Jammu were originally organised under the banner of _All Jammu and Kashmir Rajya Hindu Sabha_, with [Prem Nath Dogra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prem_Nath_Dogra "Prem Nath Dogra") as a leading member.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPuri,_The_Question_of_Accession20104-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPuri,_The_Question_of_Accession20104) In 1942, [Balraj Madhok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balraj_Madhok "Balraj Madhok") arrived in the state as a [pracharak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RSS_Pracharak "RSS Pracharak") of the [Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashtriya_Swayamsevak_Sangh "Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh") (RSS). He established branches of the RSS in Jammu and later in the Kashmir Valley. Prem Nath Dogra was also the chairman ( _sanghchalak_) of the RSS in Jammu.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot,_Religion,_Caste_and_Politics2011288,_301-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot,_Religion,_Caste_and_Politics2011288,_301)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot,_Hindu_Nationalist_Movement1996149–150-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot,_Hindu_Nationalist_Movement1996149–150) In May 1947, following the Partition plan, the Hindu Sabha threw in its support to whatever the Maharaja might decide regarding the state's status, which in effect meant support for the state's independence. However, following the communal upheaval of the Partition and the tribal invasion, its position changed to supporting the accession of the state to India and, subsequently, full integration of Jammu with India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPuri,_The_Question_of_Accession20104–5-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPuri,_The_Question_of_Accession20104–5)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012194–195-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012194–195) In November 1947, shortly after the state's accession to India, the Hindu leaders launched the [Jammu Praja Parishad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Praja_Parishad "Jammu Praja Parishad") with the objective of achieving the "full integration" of Jammu and Kashmir with India, opposing the "communist-dominated anti-Dogra government of Sheikh Abdullah."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot,_Religion,_Caste_and_Politics2011288,_301-1)[\[193\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot,_Religion,_Caste_and_Politics2011288,_301)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012195-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012195)

### Autonomy and plebiscite (1947–1953)

[Article 370](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_370 "Article 370") was drafted in the [Indian constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_constitution "Indian constitution") granting special autonomous status to the state of [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_(state) "Jammu and Kashmir (state)"), as per [Instrument of Accession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Accession_(Jammu_and_Kashmir) "Instrument of Accession (Jammu and Kashmir)"). This article specifies that the State must concur in the application of laws by Indian parliament, except those that pertain to Communications, Defence and Foreign Affairs. Central Government could not exercise its power to interfere in any other areas of governance of the state.

In a broadcast on 2 November 1947, Prime Minister Jawaharlal Nehru announced that the fate of Kashmir would ultimately be decided by the people, once law and order was established, through a referendum "held under international auspices like the United Nations."[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ray2007-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-Ray2007) A similar pledge was made by the Government of India when the Kashmir dispute was referred to the UN Security Council on 1 January 1948.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ray2007-1)[\[198\]](#refnote-Ray2007) By some accounts Mountbatten had an understanding with Nehru that a referendum on the region's future would be held later.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wynbrandt2009-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-Wynbrandt2009)

[Sheikh Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Abdullah "Sheikh Abdullah") took oath as [Prime Minister of the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "List of Chief Ministers of Jammu and Kashmir") on 17 March 1948. In 1949, the Indian government obliged Hari Singh to leave Jammu and Kashmir and yield the government to Sheikh Abdullah. [Karan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karan_Singh "Karan Singh"), the son of the erstwhile [Maharajah Hari Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hari_Singh "Hari Singh") was made the _[Sadr-i-Riyasat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadr-i-Riyasat "Sadr-i-Riyasat")_ (Constitutional [Head of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_State "Head of State")) and the Governor of the state.

[Elections were held](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Constituent_Assembly_election "1951 Jammu and Kashmir Constituent Assembly election") for the [Constituent Assembly of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "Constituent Assembly of Jammu and Kashmir") in 1951, with 75 seats allocated for the Indian administered part of Kashmir, and 25 seats left reserved for the Pakistan administered part. Sheikh Abdullah's National Conference won all 75 seats in a [rigged election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Constituent_Assembly_election#The_election "1951 Jammu and Kashmir Constituent Assembly election").[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-216)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-:10) In October 1951, [Jammu & Kashmir National Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_&_Kashmir_National_Conference "Jammu & Kashmir National Conference") under the leadership of Sheikh Abdullah formed the [Constituent Assembly of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "Constituent Assembly of Jammu and Kashmir") to formulate the Constitution of the state. Sheikh initially wanted the Constituent Assembly to decide the State's accession. But this was not agreed to by Nehru, who stated that such "underhand dealing" would be very bad, as the matter was being decided by the UN.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010190–191-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010190–191)

Sheikh Abdullah was said to have ruled the state in an undemocratic and authoritarian manner during this period.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:9-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-:9)

According to historian Zutshi, in the late 1940s, most Kashmiri Muslims in Indian Kashmir were still debating the value of the state's association with India or Pakistan. By the 1950s, she says, the National Conference government's repressive measures and the Indian state's seeming determination to settle the state's accession to India without a reference to the people of the state brought Kashmiri Muslims to extol the virtues of Pakistan and condemn India's high-handedness in its occupation of the territory, and even those who had been in India's favour began to speak in terms of the state's association with Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-220)

In early 1949, an [agitation was started](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Praja_Parishad#Jammu_agitation_(1949%E2%80%931953) "Jammu Praja Parishad") by [Jammu Praja Parishad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Praja_Parishad "Jammu Praja Parishad"), a Hindu nationalist party which was active in the Jammu region, over the ruling National Conference's policies. The government swiftly suppressed it by arresting as many as 294 members of the Praja Parishad including Prem Nath Dogra, its president. Though Sheikh's land reforms were said to have benefited the people of rural areas, Praja Parishad opposed the 'Landed Estates Abolition Act', saying it was against the Indian Constitutional rights, for implementing land acquisition without compensation. Praja Parishad also called for the full integration with the rest of India, directly clashing with the demands of National Conference for complete autonomy of the state. On 15 January 1952, students staged a demonstration against the hoisting of the state flag alongside the Indian Union flag. They were penalised, giving rise to a big procession on 8 February. The military was called out and a 72-hour curfew imposed. [N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N._Gopalaswami_Ayyangar "N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar"), the Indian Central Cabinet minister in charge of Kashmir affairs, came down to broker peace, which was resented by Sheikh Abdullah.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012195,_196-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012195,_196)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-1)[\[201\]](#refnote-:10)

In order to break the constitutional deadlock, Nehru invited the National Conference to send a delegation to Delhi. The '1952 Delhi Agreement' was formulated to settle the extent of applicability of the Indian Constitution to the Jammu and Kashmir and the relation between the State and Centre. It was reached between Nehru and Abdullah on 24 July 1952. Following this, the Constituent Assembly abolished the monarchy in Kashmir, and adopted an elected Head of State ( _Sadr-i Riyasat_). However, the Assembly was reluctant to implement the remaining measures agreed to in the Delhi Agreement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012197–203-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012197–203)[&#8202;](#ref-back-223-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-223)

In 1952, [Sheikh Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Abdullah "Sheikh Abdullah") drifted from his previous position of endorsing accession to India to insisting on the self-determination of Kashmiris.[&#8202;](#ref-back-224-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-224)

The Praja Parishad undertook a civil disobedience campaign for a third time in November 1952, which again led to repression by the state government. The Parishad accused Abdullah of communalism (sectarianism), favouring the Muslim interests in the state and sacrificing the interests of the others. The Jana Sangh joined hands with the [Hindu Mahasabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Mahasabha "Hindu Mahasabha") and [Ram Rajya Parishad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram_Rajya_Parishad "Ram Rajya Parishad") to launch a parallel agitation in Delhi. In May 1953, [Shyama Prasad Mukherjee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syama_Prasad_Mukherjee "Syama Prasad Mukherjee"), a prominent Indian leader of the time and the founder of Hindu nationalist party [Bharatiya Jana Sangh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatiya_Jana_Sangh "Bharatiya Jana Sangh") (later evolved as [BJP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatiya_Janata_Party "Bharatiya Janata Party")), made a bid to enter Jammu and Kashmir after denying to take a permit, citing his rights as an Indian citizen to visit any part of the country. Abdullah prohibited his entry and promptly arrested him when he attempted. An estimated 10,000 activists were imprisoned in Jammu, Punjab and Delhi, including Members of Parliament. Unfortunately, Mukherjee died in detention on 23 June 1953, leading to an uproar in whole India and precipitating a crisis that went out of control.[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-225)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012197–203-1)[\[206\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012197–203)

Observers state that Abdullah became upset, as he felt, his "absolute power" was being compromised in India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-226)

Meanwhile, Nehru's pledge of a referendum to people of Kashmir did not come into action. Sheikh Abdullah advocated complete independence and had allegedly joined hands with US to conspire against India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-227)

On 8 August 1953, Sheikh Abdullah was dismissed as prime minister by the _[Sadr-i-Riyasat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadr-i-Riyasat "Sadr-i-Riyasat")_ [Karan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karan_Singh "Karan Singh") on the charge that he had lost the confidence of his [cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_(government) "Cabinet (government)"). He was denied the opportunity to prove his majority on the floor of the house. He was also jailed in 1953 while Sheikh's dissident deputy, [Bakshi Ghulam Mohammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakshi_Ghulam_Mohammad "Bakshi Ghulam Mohammad") was appointed as the new [Prime Minister of the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "List of Chief Ministers of Jammu and Kashmir").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985593–607-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985593–607)

### Period of integration and rise of Kashmiri separatism (1954–1974)

> From all the information I have, 95 per cent of Kashmir Muslims do not wish to be or remain Indian citizens. I doubt therefore the wisdom of trying to keep people by force where they do not wish to stay. This cannot but have serious long-term political consequences, though immediately it may suit policy and please public opinion.

— [Jayaprakash Narayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayaprakash_Narayan "Jayaprakash Narayan")'s letter to Nehru, May 1, 1956.[&#8202;](#ref-back-229-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-229)

Bakshi Mohammad implemented all the measures of the '1952 Delhi Agreement'.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012209–212-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012209–212) In May 1954, as a subsequent to the Delhi agreement,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:22-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-:22) [The Constitution (Application to Jammu and Kashmir) Order, 1954](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Constitution_(Application_to_Jammu_and_Kashmir)_Order,_1954 "The Constitution (Application to Jammu and Kashmir) Order, 1954"), is issued by the President of India under Article 370, with the concurrence of the Government of the State of Jammu and Kashmir. In that order, the [Article 35A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_35A_of_Constitution_of_India "Article 35A of Constitution of India") is added to the Constitution of India to empower theJammu and Kashmirstate's legislature to define "permanent residents" of the state and provide special rights and privileges to those permanent residents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-:02)

On 15 February 1954, under the leadership of Bakshi Mohammad, the [Constituent Assembly of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "Constituent Assembly of Jammu and Kashmir") ratified the [state's accession to India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Accession_(Jammu_and_Kashmir) "Instrument of Accession (Jammu and Kashmir)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-233-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-233)[&#8202;](#ref-back-234-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-234) On 17 November 1956, the [Constitution of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "Constitution of Jammu and Kashmir") was adopted by the Assembly and it came into full effect on 26 January 1957.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-:6) On 24 January 1957, the UN passed a [resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_122 "United Nations Security Council Resolution 122") stating that the decisions of the Constituent Assembly would not constitute a final disposition of the State, which needs to be carried out by a free and impartial plebiscite.[&#8202;](#ref-back-236-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-236)

Meanwhile, in Pakistan administered [Azad Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Jammu_and_Kashmir "Azad Jammu and Kashmir"), the [1955 Poonch uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_Poonch_uprising "1955 Poonch uprising") begins in February 1955 against the governments dismissal of Sardar Ibrahim Khan. The rebellion would only be quashed in 1956.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-:0)

After the overthrow of Sheikh Abdullah, his lieutenant Mirza Afzal Beg formed the [Plebiscite Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plebiscite_Front "Plebiscite Front") on 9 August 1955 to fight for the plebiscite demand and the unconditional release of Sheikh Abdullah. The activities of the Plebiscite Front eventually led to the institution of the infamous [Kashmir Conspiracy Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir_Conspiracy_Case "Kashmir Conspiracy Case") in 1958 and two other cases. On 8 August 1958, Abdullah was arrested on the charges of these cases.[&#8202;](#ref-back-238-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-238)

India's Home Minister, Pandit [Govind Ballabh Pant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Govind_Ballabh_Pant "Govind Ballabh Pant"), during his visit to Srinagar in 1956, declared that the State of Jammu and Kashmir was an integral part of India and there could be no question of a plebiscite to determine its status afresh, hinting that India would resist plebiscite efforts from then on.[&#8202;](#ref-back-239-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-239)

After the mass unrest due to [missing of holy relic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theft_of_the_Holy_Relic_from_the_Hazratbal_Shrine "Theft of the Holy Relic from the Hazratbal Shrine") from the [Hazratbal Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazratbal_Shrine "Hazratbal Shrine") on 27 December 1963, the State Government dropped all charges in the [Kashmir Conspiracy Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir_Conspiracy_Case "Kashmir Conspiracy Case") as a diplomatic decision, on 8 April 1964. Sheikh Abdullah was released and returned to Srinagar where he was accorded a great welcome by the people of the valley. After his release he was reconciled with Nehru. Nehru requested Sheikh Abdullah to act as a bridge between India and Pakistan and make President [Ayub Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayub_Khan_(Field_Marshal) "Ayub Khan (Field Marshal)") of Pakistan agree to come to New Delhi for the talks for a final solution of the Kashmir problem. President Ayub Khan also sent telegrams to Nehru and Sheikh Abdullah with the message that as Pakistan too was a party to the Kashmir dispute any resolution of the conflict without its participation would not be acceptable to Pakistan. Sheikh Abdullah went to Pakistan in the spring of 1964. President Ayub Khan held extensive talks with him to explore various avenues for solving the Kashmir problem and agreed to come to [Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi "Delhi") in mid June for talks with Nehru as suggested by him. Even the date of his proposed visit was fixed and communicated to New Delhi. However, while Abdullah was still in Pakistan, news came of the sudden death of Nehru on 27 May 1964. The peace initiative died with Nehru.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985752–786-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985752–786)

After Nehru's death in 1964, Abdullah was interned from 1965 to 1968 and exiled from Kashmir in 1971 for 18 months. The [Plebiscite Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plebiscite_Front "Plebiscite Front") was also banned. This was allegedly done to prevent him and the Plebiscite Front which was supported by him, from taking part in elections in Kashmir.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985817–825-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985817–825)

On 21 November 1964, the [Articles 356 and 357](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President's_rule "President's rule") of the Indian Constitution were extended to the state, by virtue of which the Central Government can assume the government of the State and exercise its legislative powers. On 24 November 1964, the Jammu and Kashmir Assembly passed a constitutional amendment changing the elected post of _Sadr-i-Riyasat_ to a centrally-nominated post of "Governor" and renaming "Prime Minister" to "Chief Minister", which is regarded as the "end of the road" for the Article 370, and the Constitutional autonomy guaranteed by it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-1)[\[219\]](#refnote-:6) On 3 January 1965, prior to [1967 Assembly elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election "1967 Jammu and Kashmir Legislative Assembly election"), the Jammu and Kashmir National Conference dissolved itself and merged into the [Indian National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Congress "Indian National Congress"), as a marked centralising strategy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-242)

After [Indo-Pakistani War of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1965 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1965"), Kashmiri nationalists [Amanullah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanullah_Khan_(JKLF) "Amanullah Khan (JKLF)") and [Maqbool Bhat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqbool_Bhat "Maqbool Bhat"), along with [Hashim Qureshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashim_Qureshi?action=edit&redlink=1 "Hashim Qureshi (page does not exist)"), in 1966, formed another [Plebiscite Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Kashmir_Plebiscite_Front "Azad Kashmir Plebiscite Front") in Azad Kashmir with an armed wing called the [National Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Kashmir_Plebiscite_Front#National_Liberation_Front "Azad Kashmir Plebiscite Front") (NLF), with the objective of freeing Kashmir from Indian occupation and then liberating the whole of Jammu and Kashmir. Later in 1976, Maqbool Bhat is arrested on his return to the Valley. Amanullah Khan moved to England and there NLF was renamed [Jammu and Kashmir Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front "Jammu and Kashmir Liberation Front") (JKLF).

Shortly after 1965 war, Kashmiri Pandit activist and writer, [Prem Nath Bazaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prem_Nath_Bazaz "Prem Nath Bazaz") wrote that the overwhelming majority of Kashmir's Muslims were unfriendly to India and wanted to get rid of the political setup, but did not want to use violence for this purpose. He added: "It would take another quarter century of repression and generation turnover for the pacifist approach to yield decisively as armed struggle, qualifying Kashmiris as 'reluctant secessionists'."[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bose_p.84-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-Bose_p.84)

In 1966 the Indian opposition leader [Jayaprakash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayaprakash_Narayan "Jayaprakash Narayan") wrote to Indian Prime Minister Indira Gandhi that India rules Kashmir by force.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bose_p.84-1)[\[227\]](#refnote-Bose_p.84)

In 1974, the State Subject law was officially abolished in Gilgit Baltistan, which allowed any Pakistani to settle and buy land.[&#8202;](#ref-back-DawnGilgitBaltistan-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-DawnGilgitBaltistan)

### Revival of National Conference (1975–1983)

In 1971, the declaration of Bangladesh's independence was proclaimed on 26 March by Sheikh Mujibur Rahman, and subsequently the [Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War "Bangladesh Liberation War") broke out in erstwhile [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan "East Pakistan") between Pakistan and Bangladesh which was later joined by India, and subsequently [war broke out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1971") on the western border of India between India and Pakistan, both of which culminated in the creation of [Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh "Bangladesh").

It is said that, Sheikh Abdullah, watching the alarming turn of events in the subcontinent, realized that for the survival of the region, there was an urgent need to stop pursuing confrontational politics and promoting solution of issues by a process of reconciliation and dialogue. Critics of Sheikh hold the view that he gave up the cherished goal of plebiscite for gaining Chief Minister's chair. He started talks with the then Prime Minister [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi "Indira Gandhi") for normalizing the situation in the region and came to an accord with her, called [1975 Indira-Sheikh accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Indira-Sheikh_accord "1974 Indira-Sheikh accord"), by giving up the demand for a plebiscite in lieu of the people being given the right to self-rule by a democratically elected Government (as envisaged under [article 370 of the Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_370_of_the_Constitution_of_India "Article 370 of the Constitution of India")), rather than the "puppet government" which is said to have ruled the state until then.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985827–838-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985827–838) Sheikh Abdullah revived the [National Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_&_Kashmir_National_Conference "Jammu & Kashmir National Conference"), and Mirza Afzal Beg's [Plebiscite Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Plebiscite_Front "All Jammu and Kashmir Plebiscite Front") was dissolved in the [NC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_&_Kashmir_National_Conference "Jammu & Kashmir National Conference"). Sheikh assumed the position of [Chief Minister of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "List of Chief Ministers of Jammu and Kashmir") again after 11 years. Later in 1977, the Central Government and the ruling Congress Party withdrew its support so that the State Assembly had to be dissolved and mid term elections called. Sheikh's party [National Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_&_Kashmir_National_Conference "Jammu & Kashmir National Conference") won a majority (47 out of 74 seats) in the subsequent elections, on the pledge to restore Jammu and Kashmir's autonomy, and Sheikh Abdullah was re-elected as Chief Minister. The [1977 Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election "1977 Jammu and Kashmir Legislative Assembly election") is regarded as the first "free and fair" election in the Jammu and Kashmir state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-2)[\[219\]](#refnote-:6)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985860–882-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985860–882)[&#8202;](#ref-back-247-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-247)

He remained as Chief Minister of Jammu and Kashmir until his death in 1982. Later his eldest son [Farooq Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farooq_Abdullah "Farooq Abdullah") succeeded him as the [Chief Minister of the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "List of Chief Ministers of Jammu and Kashmir").

During the [1983 Assembly elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election "1983 Jammu and Kashmir Legislative Assembly election"), Indira Gandhi campaigned aggressively, raising the bogey of a 'Muslim invasion' in the Jammu region because of the Resettlement Bill, passed by the then NC government, which gave Kashmiris who left for Pakistan between 1947 and 1954 the right to return, reclaim their properties and resettle. On the other hand, Farooq Abdullah allied with the [Mirwaiz Maulvi Mohammed Farooq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirwaiz_Maulvi_Farooq "Mirwaiz Maulvi Farooq") for the elections and charged that the state's autonomy had been eroded by successive Congress Party governments. The strategies yielded dividends and the Congress won 26 seats, while the NC secured 46. Barring an odd constituency, all victories of the Congress were in the Jammu and Ladakh regions, while NC swept the Kashmir Valley. This election is said to have cemented the political polarization on religious lines in the Jammu and Kashmir state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-248-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-248)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weaver-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-Weaver)

After the results of the 1983 election, the Hindu nationalists in the state were demanding stricter central government control over the state whereas Kashmir's Muslims wanted to preserve the state's autonomy. Islamic fundamentalist groups clamoured for a plebiscite. Maulvi Farooq challenged the contention that there was no longer a dispute on Kashmir. He said that the people's movement for plebiscite would not die even though India thought it did when Sheikh Abdullah died.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weaver-1)[\[233\]](#refnote-Weaver)

In 1983, learned men of Kashmiri politics testified that Kashmiris had always wanted to be independent. But the more serious-minded among them also realised that this is not possible, considering Kashmir's size and borders.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Weaver-2)[\[233\]](#refnote-Weaver)

According to historian [Mridu Rai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mridu_Rai "Mridu Rai"), for three decades Delhi's handpicked politicians in Kashmir had supported the State's accession to India in return for generous disbursements from Delhi. Rai states that the state elections were conducted in Jammu and Kashmir, but except for the [1977](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election "1977 Jammu and Kashmir Legislative Assembly election") and [1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election "1983 Jammu and Kashmir Legislative Assembly election") elections no [state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir#Legislative_assembly_elections "Elections in Jammu and Kashmir") was fair.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMridu_Rai,_Hindu_Rulers,_Muslim_Subjects2004289-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMridu_Rai,_Hindu_Rulers,_Muslim_Subjects2004289)

Kashmiri Pandit activist Prem Nath Bazaz wrote that if free elections were held, the majority of seats would be won by those not friendly to India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bose_p.84-2)[\[227\]](#refnote-Bose_p.84)

### Rise of the separatist movement and Islamism (1984–1986)

See also: [1986 Anantnag Riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Anantnag_Riots "1986 Anantnag Riots")

Increasing anti-Indian protests took place in Kashmir in the 1980s. The Soviet-Afghan jihad and the Islamic Revolution in Iran were becoming sources of inspiration for large numbers of Kashmiri Muslim youth. The state authorities responded with increasing use of brute force to simple economic demands. Both the pro-Independence Jammu and Kashmir Liberation Front (JKLF) and the pro-Pakistan Islamist groups including JIJK mobilised the fast growing anti-Indian sentiments among the Kashmiri population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESikand2002745-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESikand2002745) 1984 saw a pronounced rise in terrorist violence in Kashmir. When [Kashmir Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Kashmir_Liberation_Front "Jammu Kashmir Liberation Front") militant [Maqbool Bhat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqbool_Bhat "Maqbool Bhat") was executed in February 1984, strikes and protests by Kashmiri nationalists broke out in the region. Large numbers of Kashmiri youth participated in widespread anti India demonstrations, which faced heavy handed reprisals by Indian state forces.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESikand2002705–751_(746-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESikand2002705–751_(746)-252)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007157-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007157) Critics of the then Chief Minister, Farooq Abdullah, charged that Abdullah was losing control. His visit to Pakistan administered Kashmir became an embarrassment, where according to [Hashim Qureshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashim_Qureshi?action=edit&redlink=1 "Hashim Qureshi (page does not exist)"), he shared a platform with Kashmir Liberation Front. Though Abdullah asserted that he went on behalf of [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi "Indira Gandhi") and his father, so that sentiments there could "be known first hand", few people believed him. There were also allegations that he had allowed [Khalistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalistan_movement "Khalistan movement") terrorist groups to train in Jammu province, although those allegations were never proved. On 2 July 1984, [Ghulam Mohammad Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghulam_Mohammad_Shah "Ghulam Mohammad Shah"), who had support from Indira Gandhi, replaced his brother-in-law Farooq Abdullah and became the chief minister of Jammu and Kashmir, after Abdullah was dismissed, in what was termed as a political "coup".[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007157-1)[\[237\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007157)

In 1986 some members of the JKLF crossed over to Pakistan to receive arms training but the Jamaat Islami Jammu Kashmir, which saw Kashmiri nationalism as contradicting Islamic universalism and its own desire for merging with Pakistan, did not support the JKLF movement. As late as that year, Jamaat member Syed Ali Shah Geelani, who later became a supporter of Kashmir's armed revolt, urged that the solution for the Kashmir issue be arrived at through peaceful and democratic means.[&#8202;](#ref-back-254-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-254) To achieve its goal of self-determination for the people of Jammu and Kashmir the Jamaat e Islami's stated position was that the Kashmir issues be resolved through constitutional means and dialogue.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESikand2002746-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESikand2002746)

Shah's administration, which did not have the people's mandate, turned to Islamists and opponents of India, notably the [Molvi Iftikhar Hussain Ansari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molvi_Iftikhar_Hussain_Ansari?action=edit&redlink=1 "Molvi Iftikhar Hussain Ansari (page does not exist)"), [Mohammad Shafi Qureshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Shafi_Qureshi "Mohammad Shafi Qureshi") and Mohinuddin Salati, to gain some legitimacy through religious sentiments. This gave political space to Islamists who previously lost overwhelmingly, allegedly due to massive rigging,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESikand2002744–745-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESikand2002744–745) in the 1983 state elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007157-2)[\[237\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007157) In 1986, Shah decided to construct a mosque within the premises of an ancient Hindu temple inside the New Civil Secretariat area in Jammu to be made available to the Muslim employees for 'Namaz'. People of Jammu took to streets to protest against this decision, which led to a Hindu-Muslim clash.[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-257) On his return to Kashmir valley in February 1986, Gul Shah retaliated and incited the Kashmiri Muslims by saying _Islam khatrey mein hey_ (trans. Islam is in danger). As a result, communal violence gripped the region, in which Hindus were targeted, especially the Kashmiri pandits, who later in the year 1990, [fled the valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exodus_of_Kashmiri_Hindus "Exodus of Kashmiri Hindus") in large numbers. During the [Anantnag riot in February 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Anantnag_riots "1986 Anantnag riots"), many houses and other properties belonging to Hindus were looted, burnt or damaged.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tikoo-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-Tikoo)[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-259)

Shah called in the army to curb the violence on the Hindus, but it had little effect. His government was dismissed on 12 March 1986, by the then Governor [Jagmohan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagmohan "Jagmohan") following communal riots in south Kashmir. This led Jagmohan to rule the state directly.

Jagmohan is said to have failed to distinguish between the secular forms and Islamist expressions of Kashmiri identity, and hence saw that identity as a threat. This failure was exploited by the Islamists of the valley, who defied the 'Hindu nationalist' policies implemented during Jagmohan's tenure, and thereby gained momentum. The political fight was hence being portrayed as a conflict between "Hindu" New Delhi (Central Government), and its efforts to impose its will in the state, and "Muslim" Kashmir, represented by political Islamists and clerics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007158-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007158) Jagmohan's pro-Hindu bias in the administration led to an increase in the appeal of the Muslim United Front.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Schofield2000-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-Schofield2000)

### 1987 state elections

Main article: [1987 Jammu and Kashmir Legislative Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election "1987 Jammu and Kashmir Legislative Assembly election")

An alliance of Islamic parties organized into [Muslim United Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_United_Front "Muslim United Front") (MUF) to contest the [1987 state elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election "1987 Jammu and Kashmir Legislative Assembly election").[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-262) Culturally, the growing emphasis on secularism led to a backlash with Islamic parties becoming more popular.[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-263) MUF's election manifesto stressed the need to solve all outstanding issues according to the [Simla agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_agreement "Simla agreement"), work for Islamic unity and against political interference from the centre. Their slogan was wanting the law of the Quran in the Assembly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-264)

There was highest recorded participation in this election. 80% of the people in the Valley voted. MUF received victory in only 4 of the contested 43 electoral constituencies despite its high vote share of 31 per cent (this means that its official vote in the Valley was larger than one-third). The elections were widespreadly believed to have been rigged by the ruling party [National Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_&_Kashmir_National_Conference "Jammu & Kashmir National Conference"), allied with the [Indian National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Congress "Indian National Congress").[&#8202;](#ref-back-j&khistory-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-j&khistory)[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-266)[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-267)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-:3) In the absence of rigging, commentators believe that the MUF could have won fifteen to twenty seats, a contention admitted by the National Conference leader [Farooq Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farooq_Abdullah "Farooq Abdullah").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-1)[\[252\]](#refnote-:3)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Brass1994-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-Brass1994) Scholar [Sumantra Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumantra_Bose "Sumantra Bose"), on the other hand. opines that the MUF would have won most of the constituencies in the Kashmir Valley.[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-270)
